<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We resolve the symmetric Mahler conjecture positively, including its equality classification. Every origin-symmetric convex body in ℝ<sup><i>n</i></sup> has volume product at least $`4^n/n!`$, with equality exactly for invertible linear images of Hanner polytopes.

- **C01** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For every integer $n\ge1$ and every origin-symmetric convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$, $$\label{eq:main-inequality} \|K\|\,\|K^\circ\|\ge\frac{4^n}{n!}.$$ Equality holds if and only if $K$ is a linear Hanner body.
  - Explanation: The volume product has the sharp symmetric lower bound, and equality characterizes linear Hanner bodies.
- **C02** (bound, [source](#stmt-cor-functional-mahler)): For every integer $n\ge1$ and every even proper lower-semicontinuous convex function $\varphi:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R\cup\{+\infty\}$ such that $0<\int_{\mathbb R^n}e^{-\varphi(x)}\,dx<\infty$, $$\left(\int_{\mathbb R^n}e^{-\varphi(x)}\,dx\right) \left(\int_{\mathbb R^n}e^{-\varphi^*(y)}\,dy\right)\ge4^n.$$ The second integral is allowed to be infinite, in which case the inequality is immediate.
  - Explanation: Even convex potentials satisfy a sharp functional volume-product lower bound; an infinite conjugate integral is permitted.
- **C03** (bound, [source](#stmt-cor-entropy-transport)): For every integer $n\ge1$, let $\eta_i(dx)=e^{-V_i(x)}\,dx$, $i=1,2$, be full-dimensional log-concave probability measures, both symmetric about the origin, with proper lower-semicontinuous convex potentials $V_i$. Suppose their densities are essentially continuous, meaning that $e^{-V_i}=0$ at $\mathcal H^{n-1}$-almost every point of $\partial\operatorname{supp}\eta_i$. Their moment measures $\nu_i=(\nabla V_i)_\#\eta_i$ have finite first moments, $H(\eta_i)=-\int V_i\,d\eta_i$ is finite, and $$H(\eta_1)+H(\eta_2)\le -n\log(4e^2)+\mathcal T(\nu_1,\nu_2).$$
  - Explanation: Essentially continuous symmetric log-concave probability densities satisfy the stated entropy–transport bound, with finite entropy and moment-measure first moments.
- **C04** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-sums)): For origin-symmetric convex bodies of dimensions $k,l\ge1$, $$\label{eq:sum-polars} (A\oplus_1 A')^\circ=A^\circ\oplus_\infty(A')^\circ, \qquad (A\oplus_\infty A')^\circ=A^\circ\oplus_1(A')^\circ.$$ Moreover, $$\label{eq:sum-volumes} \|A\oplus_\infty A'\|=\|A\|\,\|A'\|, \qquad \|A\oplus_1 A'\|=\frac{k!\,l!}{(k+l)!}\|A\|\,\|A'\|.$$ Consequently, for $s\in\{1,\infty\}$, $$\label{eq:sum-products} P(A\oplus_s A')=\frac{P(A)P(A')}{\binom{k+l}{k}}.$$
  - Explanation: Polarity exchanges the two norm sums, and their volumes give the common volume-product recursion.
- **C05** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-hanner)): Every $d$-dimensional linear Hanner body $H$ satisfies $$P(H)=\frac{4^d}{d!}.$$ The polar of a linear Hanner body is again a linear Hanner body.
  - Explanation: Linear Hanner bodies attain the sharp volume product and are closed under polarity.
- **C06** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-planar-lens)): The function $F$ is a biholomorphism from $\mathbb D$ onto a bounded convex domain $D$, with inverse $g$, and $F'(0)=8/\pi^2$. There is a continuous even function $\lambda:[-1,1]\to[0,\infty)$, positive on $(-1,1)$ and zero at $\pm1$, such that $$\label{eq:lens-domain} \overline D=\{v+it:\|t\|\leq1,\ \|v\|\leq\lambda(t)\}.$$ The function $\lambda$ is smooth on $(-1,1)$ and satisfies $$\label{eq:lens-width-curvature} \lambda'(t)=-\frac2\pi \operatorname{arctanh}\!\left(\sin\frac{\pi t}{2}\right), \qquad \lambda''(t)=-\sec\frac{\pi t}{2}<0.$$ If $\Theta$ is uniform on $[0,2\pi)$, then $$\label{eq:lens-boundary-law} F(e^{i\Theta})\ \stackrel{\mathrm{law}}{=}\ \varepsilon\lambda(T)+iT,$$ where $T$ is uniform on $[-1,1]$ and $\varepsilon$ is an independent uniform sign.
  - Explanation: The conformal lens has strictly concave horizontal width and a uniform imaginary boundary coordinate with an independent fair branch sign.
- **C07** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-lens-endpoint)): As $\eta\downarrow0$, $$\label{eq:lens-endpoint-asymptotic} \lambda(1-\eta)=\frac2\pi\eta\log\frac1\eta+O(\eta).$$ For every $0<a\leq b<\infty$, $$\label{eq:lens-endpoint-ratio} \sup_{a\leq c\leq b} \left\|\frac{\lambda(1-\delta c)}{\lambda(1-\delta)}-c\right\| \longrightarrow0\qquad(\delta\downarrow0).$$
  - Explanation: The lens width has logarithmic endpoint scaling, with the ratio limit uniform on each prescribed compact positive parameter interval.
- **C08** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-holomorphic-mass)): Let $d\geq1$, $N\geq d$, and let $\mathcal U\subset\mathbb C^d$ be open and contain $0$. Suppose that $f=(f_1,\ldots,f_N):\mathcal U\to\mathbb C^N$ is holomorphic and has the following properties:* 1. *$f^{-1}(0)=\{0\}$;* 2. *for an integer $k\geq1$, $$f(z)=H(z)+O(\|z\|^{k+1})\quad\text{near }0,$$ where each component of $H$ is a homogeneous polynomial of degree $k$ and $H(z)\ne0$ whenever $z\ne0$;* 3. *for every $0<s<1$, the set $\{z\in\mathcal U:\|f(z)\|^2\leq s\}$ is compact in $\mathcal U$.* *Then, with $\tau=\|f\|^2$, $$\label{eq:holomorphic-minor-mass} \int_{\{\tau<1\}} \sum_{\substack{I\subset\{1,\ldots,N\}\\\|I\|=d}} \left\|\det\left(\frac{\partial f_i}{\partial z_j}\right)_{ i\in I,\ 1\leq j\leq d}\right\|^2\,dV \geq \frac{(\pi k)^d}{d!}.$$ All norms are Euclidean. Rows in each determinant are in increasing index order.
  - Explanation: An isolated homogeneous leading zero and compact sublevels force the lower bound on the integral of squared holomorphic Jacobian minors.
- **C09** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-holomorphic-feasibility)): Let $d\ge1$ and let $b_1,\ldots,b_m$ be nonzero real rows spanning $(\mathbb R^d)^*$. Let $F:\mathbb D\to D$ be a biholomorphism onto a bounded planar domain, continuous on $\overline{\mathbb D}$, with $F(0)=0$. Then the sum $S$ in <a href="#eq:feasible-probabilities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:feasible-probabilities">[eq:feasible-probabilities]</a> satisfies $S\ge1$. No condition on extra-row boundary equalities or on dependent row subsets is required.
  - Explanation: The sum of boundary feasibility probabilities over independent row bases is bounded below without excluding boundary ties.
- **C10** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-boundary-ties)): Let $F:\mathbb D\to D$ be the lens map of Lemma <a href="#lem:planar-lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:planar-lens">6</a>. Assume that the nonzero spanning rows $b_1,\ldots,b_m$ are pairwise nonproportional. For every $I\in\mathcal B$ and $j\notin I$, $$\mathbb P\{b_jZ_I(\theta)\in\partial D\}=0.$$
  - Explanation: Pairwise nonproportional spanning rows make every additional lens boundary equality a null event.
- **C11** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-simplex-integral)): Let $b_1,\ldots,b_m$ be nonzero, pairwise nonproportional real rows spanning $(\mathbb R^d)^*$, and let $F,D,\lambda$ be as in Lemma <a href="#lem:planar-lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:planar-lens">6</a>. With $A$, $S$, and $\Sigma_X$ defined in <a href="#eq:strip-body" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:strip-body">[eq:strip-body]</a>, <a href="#eq:feasible-probabilities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:feasible-probabilities">[eq:feasible-probabilities]</a>, and <a href="#eq:feasible-simplex-union" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:feasible-simplex-union">[eq:feasible-simplex-union]</a>, respectively, $$\label{eq:simplex-integral} 1\leq S =\frac{d!}{4^d}\int_A\|\Sigma_X\|\,dX \leq\frac{d!}{4^d}\|A\|\,\|A^\circ\|.$$ In particular, $\|A\|\,\|A^\circ\|\geq4^d/d!$, and $$\label{eq:simplex-deficit} 0\leq\int_A\bigl(\|A^\circ\|-\|\Sigma_X\|\bigr)\,dX \leq \|A\|\,\|A^\circ\|-\frac{4^d}{d!}.$$
  - Explanation: The boundary probability sum equals an integrated feasible-simplex volume and bounds the missing polar volume.
- **C12** (bound, [source](#stmt-cor-symmetric-inequality)): For every origin-symmetric convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^d$, $d\geq1$, $$\|K\|\,\|K^\circ\|\geq\frac{4^d}{d!}.$$
  - Explanation: The symmetric volume-product inequality holds for arbitrary convex bodies through polytope approximation.
- **C13** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-optimal-representation)): Let $K\subset\mathbb R^n$, $n\ge1$, be an origin-symmetric convex body with $P(K)=4^n/n!$. Put $Q=K^\circ$, $d=n+1$, and define the lifted bodies $B,C$ by <a href="#eq:segment-lift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:segment-lift">[eq:segment-lift]</a>. For every $X\in\operatorname{int}B$ and every nonzero $\xi=(q,a)\in\mathbb R^n\times\mathbb R$, put $$T=p_C(\xi)=\max\{p_Q(q),\|a\|\}.$$ There are $1\le r\le d$ vectors $c_i=(b_i,\sigma_i)$ with $b_i\in Q$ and $\sigma_i\in\{-1,1\}$, nonnegative numbers $\alpha_i$, and a vector $Y\in\mathbb R^d$, such that $$\label{eq:optimal-representation} \xi=\sum_{i=1}^r\alpha_i c_i, \qquad \sum_{i=1}^r\alpha_i=T,$$ and $$\label{eq:common-witness} \|h\cdot Y\|\le\lambda(h\cdot X)\quad(h\in C), \qquad c_i\cdot Y=\lambda(c_i\cdot X)\quad(1\le i\le r).$$ In particular, for every finite representation $\xi=\sum_{j=1}^s\beta_jh_j$ with $\beta_j\ge0$ and $h_j\in C$, $$\label{eq:optimal-cost} \sum_{i=1}^r\alpha_i\lambda(c_i\cdot X) \le \sum_{j=1}^s\beta_j\lambda(h_j\cdot X).$$ The choices may depend on $X$ and $\xi$; the same choice satisfies <a href="#eq:optimal-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:optimal-cost">[eq:optimal-cost]</a> for all the competing representations.
  - Explanation: An equality body admits a positive representation with gauge-sized total weight and a common witness minimizing lens cost against every finite positive competitor.
- **C14** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-metric-median)): Let $K\subset\mathbb R^n$, $n\ge1$, be an origin-symmetric convex body with $P(K)=4^n/n!$, and put $Q=K^\circ$. Every triple of points in $(\mathbb R^n,p_Q)$ has a metric median.
  - Explanation: Equality in the symmetric volume product forces a metric median for every triple in the polar gauge norm.
- **C15** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-three-balls)): Suppose every triple of points in a real normed space has a metric median. Then the space has the three-ball intersection property, including balls of radius zero.
  - Explanation: Metric medians imply a common intersection for pairwise-intersecting closed balls, including zero radii.
- **C16** (classification, [source](#stmt-thm-hansen-lima)): A nonzero finite-dimensional real Banach space with the 3.2 intersection property is linearly isometric to a space obtained from the real line by finitely many $\ell_1$ and $\ell_\infty$ direct sums.
  - Explanation: The ball intersection property classifies finite-dimensional real Banach spaces as recursively formed norm sums of the real line.
- **C17** (classification, [source](#stmt-prop-equality-classification)): If an origin-symmetric convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$, $n\ge1$, satisfies $P(K)=4^n/n!$, then it is a linear Hanner body.
  - Explanation: Every symmetric equality body is an invertible linear image of a Hanner polytope.

      "quantifiers": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-thm-main",
          "exact_quote": "For every integer $n\\ge1$ and every origin-symmetric convex body $K\\subset\\mathbb R^n$, $$\\label{eq:main-inequality}\n |K|\\,|K^\\circ|\\ge\\frac{4^n}{n!}.$$ Equality holds if and only if $K$ is a linear Hanner body.",
          "source_id": "PM-087.1"
        }
      ],
      "statement_anchor": "stmt-thm-main"
    },
    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-cor-functional-mahler",
        "exact_quote": "For every integer $n\\ge1$ and every even proper lower-semicontinuous convex function $\\varphi:\\mathbb R^n\\to\\mathbb R\\cup\\{+\\infty\\}$ such that $0<\\int_{\\mathbb R^n}e^{-\\varphi(x)}\\,dx<\\infty$, $$\\left(\\int_{\\mathbb R^n}e^{-\\varphi(x)}\\,dx\\right)\n \\left(\\int_{\\mathbb R^n}e^{-\\varphi^*(y)}\\,dy\\right)\\ge4^n.$$ The second integral is allowed to be infinite, in which case the inequality is immediate.",
        "source_id": "PM-087.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-cor-functional-mahler",
          "exact_quote": "For every integer $n\\ge1$ and every even proper lower-semicontinuous convex function $\\varphi:\\mathbb R^n\\to\\mathbb R\\cup\\{+\\infty\\}$ such that $0<\\int_{\\mathbb R^n}e^{-\\varphi(x)}\\,dx<\\infty$, $$\\left(\\int_{\\mathbb R^n}e^{-\\varphi(x)}\\,dx\\right)\n \\left(\\int_{\\mathbb R^n}e^{-\\varphi^*(y)}\\,dy\\right)\\ge4^n.$$ The second integral is allowed to be infinite, in which case the inequality is immediate.",
          "source_id": "PM-087.1"
        }

      "exact_statement": "For every integer $n\\ge1$ and every even proper lower-semicontinuous convex function $\\varphi:\\mathbb R^n\\to\\mathbb R\\cup\\{+\\infty\\}$ such that $0<\\int_{\\mathbb R^n}e^{-\\varphi(x)}\\,dx<\\infty$, $$\\left(\\int_{\\mathbb R^n}e^{-\\varphi(x)}\\,dx\\right)\n \\left(\\int_{\\mathbb R^n}e^{-\\varphi^*(y)}\\,dy\\right)\\ge4^n.$$ The second integral is allowed to be infinite, in which case the inequality is immediate.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-cor-entropy-transport",
        "exact_quote": "For every integer $n\\ge1$, let $\\eta_i(dx)=e^{-V_i(x)}\\,dx$, $i=1,2$, be full-dimensional log-concave probability measures, both symmetric about the origin, with proper lower-semicontinuous convex potentials $V_i$. Suppose their densities are essentially continuous, meaning that $e^{-V_i}=0$ at $\\mathcal H^{n-1}$-almost every point of $\\partial\\operatorname{supp}\\eta_i$. Their moment measures $\\nu_i=(\\nabla V_i)_\\#\\eta_i$ have finite first moments, $H(\\eta_i)=-\\int V_i\\,d\\eta_i$ is finite, and $$H(\\eta_1)+H(\\eta_2)\\le -n\\log(4e^2)+\\mathcal T(\\nu_1,\\nu_2).$$",
        "source_id": "PM-087.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-cor-entropy-transport",
          "exact_quote": "For every integer $n\\ge1$, let $\\eta_i(dx)=e^{-V_i(x)}\\,dx$, $i=1,2$, be full-dimensional log-concave probability measures, both symmetric about the origin, with proper lower-semicontinuous convex potentials $V_i$. Suppose their densities are essentially continuous, meaning that $e^{-V_i}=0$ at $\\mathcal H^{n-1}$-almost every point of $\\partial\\operatorname{supp}\\eta_i$. Their moment measures $\\nu_i=(\\nabla V_i)_\\#\\eta_i$ have finite first moments, $H(\\eta_i)=-\\int V_i\\,d\\eta_i$ is finite, and $$H(\\eta_1)+H(\\eta_2)\\le -n\\log(4e^2)+\\mathcal T(\\nu_1,\\nu_2).$$",
          "source_id": "PM-087.1"
        }
      ],

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-sums",
        "exact_quote": "For origin-symmetric convex bodies of dimensions $k,l\\ge1$, $$\\label{eq:sum-polars}\n (A\\oplus_1 A')^\\circ=A^\\circ\\oplus_\\infty(A')^\\circ,\n \\qquad\n (A\\oplus_\\infty A')^\\circ=A^\\circ\\oplus_1(A')^\\circ.$$ Moreover, $$\\label{eq:sum-volumes}\n |A\\oplus_\\infty A'|=|A|\\,|A'|,\n \\qquad\n |A\\oplus_1 A'|=\\frac{k!\\,l!}{(k+l)!}|A|\\,|A'|.$$ Consequently, for $s\\in\\{1,\\infty\\}$, $$\\label{eq:sum-products}\n P(A\\oplus_s A')=\\frac{P(A)P(A')}{\\binom{k+l}{k}}.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-087.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-sums",
          "exact_quote": "For origin-symmetric convex bodies of dimensions $k,l\\ge1$, $$\\label{eq:sum-polars}\n (A\\oplus_1 A')^\\circ=A^\\circ\\oplus_\\infty(A')^\\circ,\n \\qquad\n (A\\oplus_\\infty A')^\\circ=A^\\circ\\oplus_1(A')^\\circ.$$ Moreover, $$\\label{eq:sum-volumes}\n |A\\oplus_\\infty A'|=|A|\\,|A'|,\n \\qquad\n |A\\oplus_1 A'|=\\frac{k!\\,l!}{(k+l)!}|A|\\,|A'|.$$ Consequently, for $s\\in\\{1,\\infty\\}$, $$\\label{eq:sum-products}\n P(A\\oplus_s A')=\\frac{P(A)P(A')}{\\binom{k+l}{k}}.$$",
          "source_id": "PM-087.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For origin-symmetric convex bodies of dimensions $k,l\\ge1$, $$\\label{eq:sum-polars}\n (A\\oplus_1 A')^\\circ=A^\\circ\\oplus_\\infty(A')^\\circ,\n \\qquad\n (A\\oplus_\\infty A')^\\circ=A^\\circ\\oplus_1(A')^\\circ.$$ Moreover, $$\\label{eq:sum-volumes}\n |A\\oplus_\\infty A'|=|A|\\,|A'|,\n \\qquad\n |A\\oplus_1 A'|=\\frac{k!\\,l!}{(k+l)!}|A|\\,|A'|.$$ Consequently, for $s\\in\\{1,\\infty\\}$, $$\\label{eq:sum-products}\n P(A\\oplus_s A')=\\frac{P(A)P(A')}{\\binom{k+l}{k}}.$$",

      "conclusion": {

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-planar-lens",
        "exact_quote": "The function $F$ is a biholomorphism from $\\mathbb D$ onto a bounded convex domain $D$, with inverse $g$, and $F'(0)=8/\\pi^2$. There is a continuous even function $\\lambda:[-1,1]\\to[0,\\infty)$, positive on $(-1,1)$ and zero at $\\pm1$, such that $$\\label{eq:lens-domain}\n \\overline D=\\{v+it:|t|\\leq1,\\ |v|\\leq\\lambda(t)\\}.$$ The function $\\lambda$ is smooth on $(-1,1)$ and satisfies $$\\label{eq:lens-width-curvature}\n \\lambda'(t)=-\\frac2\\pi\n       \\operatorname{arctanh}\\!\\left(\\sin\\frac{\\pi t}{2}\\right),\n \\qquad\n \\lambda''(t)=-\\sec\\frac{\\pi t}{2}<0.$$ If $\\Theta$ is uniform on $[0,2\\pi)$, then $$\\label{eq:lens-boundary-law}\n F(e^{i\\Theta})\\ \\stackrel{\\mathrm{law}}{=}\\\n \\varepsilon\\lambda(T)+iT,$$ where $T$ is uniform on $[-1,1]$ and $\\varepsilon$ is an independent uniform sign.",
        "source_id": "PM-087.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-planar-lens",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-lens-endpoint",
        "exact_quote": "As $\\eta\\downarrow0$, $$\\label{eq:lens-endpoint-asymptotic}\n \\lambda(1-\\eta)=\\frac2\\pi\\eta\\log\\frac1\\eta+O(\\eta).$$ For every $0<a\\leq b<\\infty$, $$\\label{eq:lens-endpoint-ratio}\n \\sup_{a\\leq c\\leq b}\n \\left|\\frac{\\lambda(1-\\delta c)}{\\lambda(1-\\delta)}-c\\right|\n \\longrightarrow0\\qquad(\\delta\\downarrow0).$$",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-holomorphic-mass",
        "exact_quote": "Let $d\\geq1$, $N\\geq d$, and let $\\mathcal U\\subset\\mathbb C^d$ be open and contain $0$. Suppose that $f=(f_1,\\ldots,f_N):\\mathcal U\\to\\mathbb C^N$ is holomorphic and has the following properties:*\n\n1.  *$f^{-1}(0)=\\{0\\}$;*\n\n2.  *for an integer $k\\geq1$, $$f(z)=H(z)+O(|z|^{k+1})\\quad\\text{near }0,$$ where each component of $H$ is a homogeneous polynomial of degree $k$ and $H(z)\\ne0$ whenever $z\\ne0$;*\n\n3.  *for every $0<s<1$, the set $\\{z\\in\\mathcal U:|f(z)|^2\\leq s\\}$ is compact in $\\mathcal U$.*\n\n\n*Then, with $\\tau=|f|^2$, $$\\label{eq:holomorphic-minor-mass}\n\\int_{\\{\\tau<1\\}}\n \\sum_{\\substack{I\\subset\\{1,\\ldots,N\\}\\\\|I|=d}}\n \\left|\\det\\left(\\frac{\\partial f_i}{\\partial z_j}\\right)_{\n                       i\\in I,\\ 1\\leq j\\leq d}\\right|^2\\,dV\n\\geq \\frac{(\\pi k)^d}{d!}.$$ All norms are Euclidean. Rows in each determinant are in increasing index order.",
        "source_id": "PM-087.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-holomorphic-mass",
          "exact_quote": "Let $d\\geq1$, $N\\geq d$, and let $\\mathcal U\\subset\\mathbb C^d$ be open and contain $0$.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-holomorphic-feasibility",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **convex body** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A convex body is a compact convex subset of $\mathbb R^n$ with nonempty interior.
- **polar body** ([source](#sec:introduction)): If $K=-K$, its center $0$ is an interior point, and its polar body is $$K^\circ=\{y\in\mathbb R^n:\langle x,y\rangle\le1 \text{ for every }x\in K\}.$$
- **volume product** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Write $\|K\|$ for $n$-dimensional Lebesgue volume and $P(K)=\|K\|\,\|K^\circ\|$ for the volume product.
- **Hanner polytope** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A centered nondegenerate interval is a one-dimensional *Hanner polytope*.
- **Hanner recursion** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Given Hanner polytopes $H_1\subset\mathbb R^k$ and $H_2\subset\mathbb R^l$, their Cartesian product and the convex hull $$\begin{aligned} H_1\oplus_\infty H_2&=H_1\times H_2,\\ H_1\oplus_1 H_2&= \mathop{\mathrm{conv}}\bigl((H_1\times\{0\})\cup(\{0\}\times H_2)\bigr) \end{aligned}$$ are Hanner polytopes in $\mathbb R^{k+l}$.
- **linear Hanner body** ([source](#sec:introduction)): We call an invertible linear image of a Hanner polytope a *linear Hanner body*.
- **Fenchel–Legendre transform** ([source](#sec:functional-mahler)): For a proper lower-semicontinuous convex function $\varphi:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R\cup\{+\infty\}$, its Fenchel–Legendre transform is $$\varphi^*(y)=\sup_{x\in\mathbb R^n}\{\langle x,y\rangle-\varphi(x)\}, \qquad y\in\mathbb R^n.$$
- **gauge and support function** ([source](#eq:gauge-support)): In addition to the polar and volume product defined in Section <a href="#sec:introduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:introduction">1</a>, we use the gauge and support function $$p_A(x)=\inf\{t>0:x\in tA\},\qquad h_A(y)=\max_{x\in A}\langle x,y\rangle.$$
- **lens map** ([source](#eq:lens-map)): $$\label{eq:lens-map} F(z)=\frac8{\pi^2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty} \frac{(-1)^jz^{2j+1}}{(2j+1)^2}.$$
- **metric median** ([source](#sec:median)): A *metric median* of three points in a normed space is a point that lies between every pair: if the points are $x_0,x_1,x_2$, a median $m$ satisfies $$\\|x_i-x_j\\|=\\|x_i-m\\|+\\|m-x_j\\| \qquad(0\le i<j\le2).$$
- **three-ball intersection property** ([source](#pm-h-24)): A normed space has the *three-ball intersection property*, also called the *3.2 intersection property*, if any three closed balls that intersect pairwise have a common point.

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| symmetric-product | symmetric volume product | ≥ | $\frac{4^n}{n!}$ | $n\ge1$ |
| hanner-product | Hanner volume product | = | $\frac{4^d}{d!}$ | The dimension of the body. |
| functional-product | even functional volume product | ≥ | $4^n$ | $n\ge1$ |
| entropy-transport | sum of relative entropies | ≤ | $-n\log(4e^2)+\mathcal T(\nu_1,\nu_2)$ | $n\ge1$ |
| sum-volume | volume of the convex-hull norm sum | = | $\frac{k!\,l!}{(k+l)!}\|A\|\,\|A'\|$ | $k,l\ge1$ |
| sum-product | volume product of either norm sum | = | $\frac{P(A)P(A')}{\binom{k+l}{k}}$ | $s\in\{1,\infty\}$ and $k,l\ge1$ |
| lens-derivative | lens derivative at the origin | = | $8/\pi^2$ | At the origin. |
| simplex-deficit | integrated missing polar volume | ≤ | $\|A\|\,\|A^\circ\|-\frac{4^d}{d!}$ | $d\ge1$ |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **hanner** [claim](#stmt-lem-hanner) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hanner): Induction using polar exchange and the norm-sum volume-product recursion. -> Hanner bodies attain equality.
- **lens** [claim](#stmt-lem-planar-lens) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-planar-lens): Analyze the holomorphic derivative and boundary parametrization. -> A convex conformal lens with uniform imaginary boundary coordinate.
- **endpoint** [claim](#stmt-lem-lens-endpoint) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-lens-endpoint): Integrate the endpoint derivative and control the remainder uniformly. -> Uniform positive-parameter endpoint ratio scaling.
- **mass** [claim](#stmt-lem-holomorphic-mass) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-holomorphic-mass): Stokes flux comparison, homogeneous phase invariance, and Cauchy–Binet. -> A lower bound on integrated squared Jacobian minors.
- **feasibility** [claim](#stmt-lem-holomorphic-feasibility) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-holomorphic-feasibility): Apply the mass bound to high inverse-map powers and use radial substitution and upper Fatou. -> The feasibility probability sum is bounded below.
- **ties** [claim](#stmt-lem-boundary-ties) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-boundary-ties): Use strict turning of tangents, local inverse charts, and Fubini. -> Additional boundary ties have null probability.
- **simplex** [claim](#stmt-prop-simplex-integral) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-simplex-integral): Change boundary coordinates to real variables and sum disjoint simplex volumes. -> The simplex identity and integrated deficit bound.
- **approximation** [claim](#stmt-cor-symmetric-inequality) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-symmetric-inequality): Approximate the polar by finite symmetric nets and pass through homothetic volume bounds. -> The inequality for arbitrary symmetric convex bodies.
- **witness** [claim](#stmt-prop-optimal-representation) / [proof](#sec:zero-deficit): Lift by a segment, use vanishing missing volume on product neighborhoods, and extract compact limits. -> A common feasibility witness and a minimal lens-cost representation.
- **median** [claim](#stmt-prop-metric-median) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-metric-median): Compare endpoint costs and use support-function separation. -> Metric medians in the polar gauge norm.
- **balls** [claim](#stmt-lem-three-balls) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-three-balls): Move a median toward a center of a ball excluding it. -> A common intersection of pairwise-intersecting closed balls.
- **classification** [claim](#stmt-prop-equality-classification) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-equality-classification): Apply the finite-dimensional intersection classification and take polars. -> Every equality body is a linear Hanner body.
- **functional** [claim](#stmt-cor-functional-mahler) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-functional-mahler): Apply the cited all-dimensional geometric-to-functional implication and convex conjugation. -> The even functional inequality.
- **entropy** [claim](#stmt-cor-entropy-transport) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-entropy-transport): Use integrability of gradients and potentials and the cited functional entropy–transport equivalence. -> The entropy–transport inequality.

        }

          "exact_quote": "For every integer $n\\ge1$, let $\\eta_i(dx)=e^{-V_i(x)}\\,dx$, $i=1,2$, be full-dimensional log-concave probability measures, both symmetric about the origin, with proper lower-semicontinuous convex potentials $V_i$. Suppose their densities are essentially continuous, meaning that $e^{-V_i}=0$ at $\\mathcal H^{n-1}$-almost every point of $\\partial\\operatorname{supp}\\eta_i$. Their moment measures $\\nu_i=(\\nabla V_i)_\\#\\eta_i$ have finite first moments, $H(\\eta_i)=-\\int V_i\\,d\\eta_i$ is finite, and $$H(\\eta_1)+H(\\eta_2)\\le -n\\log(4e^2)+\\mathcal T(\\nu_1,\\nu_2).$$",
          "source_id": "PM-087.1"
        }
      ],

    }

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **sum-volumes**: Cartesian-product volume multiplies the input volumes; the convex-hull sum multiplies them by the dimension-dependent factorial ratio.
- **linear-invariance**: The volume product is preserved under an invertible linear change of coordinates.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **geometry-preconditions**: Treat a scalar volume computation as validation of arbitrary geometric inputs. -> The sum identities require convex bodies and the stated symmetry and dimensions; supplied scalars do not establish these hypotheses.
- **numerical-equality**: Classify a body from an approximately minimal computed volume product. -> Approximate agreement does not establish the exact equality premise.
- **singular-map**: Apply volume-product invariance to a singular coordinate map. -> A singular image may lose nonempty interior in the ambient space, and reciprocal determinant cancellation is unavailable.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The norm-sum volume formulas give a finite exact computation from dimensions and volumes. The harness checks these scalar formulas; it does not verify arbitrary convex bodies or the analytic proof of the symmetric inequality.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON [k,l,v,w], with volumes encoded as rational strings.
# PRECONDITIONS: k,l are positive integers at most 4; v,w are positive rationals; bodies are origin-symmetric convex bodies in complementary spaces.
# OUTPUTS: JSON rational strings for Cartesian-product and convex-hull-sum volumes, or INVALID for rejected scalar inputs.
# TIME: O(k+l) arithmetic operations, excluding input parsing and rational bit complexity.
# SPACE: O(k+l) integer bit storage at bounded dimensions, plus input rational storage.
# ARITHMETIC: Exact integer factorials and python-flint fmpq rational arithmetic.
# FAILURE: INVALID on malformed scalar inputs; geometric hypotheses must be checked externally.
import json, sys
from math import factorial
from flint import fmpq
try:
    data = json.load(sys.stdin)
    if not isinstance(data, list) or len(data) != 4:
        raise ValueError('shape')
    k, l, v, w = data
    if type(k) is not int or type(l) is not int or not (1 <= k <= 4 and 1 <= l <= 4):
        raise ValueError('dimensions')
    if not isinstance(v, str) or not isinstance(w, str):
        raise ValueError('rational strings')
    a, b = fmpq(v), fmpq(w)
    if a <= 0 or b <= 0:
        raise ValueError('volume')
    product = a * b
    coefficient = fmpq(factorial(k) * factorial(l), factorial(k + l))
    hull = coefficient * product
    print(json.dumps([str(product), str(hull)]))
except Exception:
    print('INVALID')
```

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-01** (hypothesis): The sharp geometric bound applies to origin-symmetric convex bodies in real space with nonempty interior. Blocked use: ['Applying the symmetric bound to a nonsymmetric set, a nonconvex set, or a set with empty interior.']
- **limit-02** (hypothesis): Origin symmetry is required throughout the geometric argument; the general centered problem does not supply the symmetric theorem. Blocked use: ['Replacing origin symmetry by a freely chosen center without a separate argument.']
- **limit-03** (hypothesis): Volume-product invariance requires an invertible linear change of coordinates. Blocked use: ['Using a singular projection as an invariant coordinate change.', 'Assuming that each individual volume is invariant.']
- **limit-04** (hypothesis): The Hanner equality classification requires exact attainment of the sharp volume product. Blocked use: ['Classifying a nearly minimizing body as a linear Hanner body from numerical agreement alone.']
- **limit-05** (hypothesis): The functional inequality requires an even proper lower-semicontinuous convex potential and a finite positive primal integral. Blocked use: ['Applying the even functional bound without verifying evenness or integrability.']
- **limit-06** (edge_case): The dual functional integral may be infinite; the inequality does not guarantee a finite dual integral. Blocked use: ['Inferring dual integrability from primal integrability alone.']
- **limit-07** (formal_scope): The functional consequence uses the geometric inequality in all dimensions through a limiting implication. Blocked use: ['Deriving the functional inequality from only the geometric theorem in the dimension of the potential.']
- **limit-08** (unknown): An equality classification for even potentials is not supplied by the limiting geometric implication. Blocked use: ['Transferring the Hanner classification directly to all equality-attaining even potentials.']
- **limit-09** (hypothesis): The entropy–transport statement requires symmetric full-dimensional log-concave probability measures with the stated convex potentials and essentially continuous densities. Blocked use: ['Applying the entropy conclusion to densities with unverified boundary behavior or to measures without the stated symmetry.']
- **limit-10** (hypothesis): Spanning nonzero rows are required for boundary feasibility. Pairwise nonproportionality is an additional hypothesis of the lens boundary-tie and real simplex arguments. Blocked use: ['Using the stated simplex identity with unreduced proportional rows.', 'Imposing the boundary-tie restriction as a necessary hypothesis of the general boundary feasibility inequality.']
- **limit-11** (edge_case): The sum of individual feasible simplex volumes equals the union volume only outside the null set of additional tight constraints used in the proof. Blocked use: ['Treating the almost-everywhere simplex volume sum as a pointwise certificate at a degenerate interior point.']
- **limit-12** (approximation): The extension to arbitrary convex bodies passes through converging symmetric polytope approximations; the limiting inequality is not itself a finite volume-computation procedure. Blocked use: ['Treating a single finite approximation as an exact volume or equality certificate for an arbitrary convex body.']
- **limit-13** (nonconstructive): The common feasibility witness is obtained by subsequence compactness and need not have an independent limiting row basis. Blocked use: ['Reading the representation theorem as an algorithm with guaranteed termination for arbitrary convex-body inputs.', 'Requiring limiting rows to remain independent.']
- **limit-14** (approximation): The endpoint ratio is uniform over a fixed compact interval of positive scale factors; it does not assert uniformity as the scale factor approaches zero or becomes unbounded. Blocked use: ['Extending the endpoint ratio to scale factors outside the stated fixed positive range.']
- **limit-15** (formal_scope): The cited structural classification is for nonzero finite-dimensional real Banach spaces with the stated intersection property. Blocked use: ['Extending the classification to infinite-dimensional or complex spaces from this statement.']
- **limit-16** (edge_case): Metric medians are not asserted to be unique, and the ball-intersection conclusion includes balls of radius zero. Blocked use: ['Assuming a canonical unique median.', 'Excluding zero-radius balls as a necessary hypothesis.']

### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 087 (`lean/docs/087.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [The symmetric Mahler conjecture and its equality cases](../../preprints/The-symmetric-Mahler-conjecture-and-its-equality-cases-September-22-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [The Mahler Conjecture for General Convex Bodies](../../preprints/The-Mahler-Conjecture-for-General-Convex-Bodies-September-22-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [Symplectic Balls in Symmetric Polar Products](../../preprints/Symplectic-Balls-in-Symmetric-Polar-Products-September-22-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The symmetric Mahler conjecture predicts $|K||K^\circ|\ge4^n/n!$ for every origin-symmetric convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$. The formalization establishes this for every $n\ge1$ and characterizes equality exactly by invertible linear images of Hanner bodies, built from intervals using Cartesian products and convex-hull joins.
>
> The nonsymmetric Mahler conjecture and the functional inequalities are not included.
>
> The general Mahler conjecture gives a sharp lower bound for the volume product of a convex body and its polar. For every $n\ge1$ and compact convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$ with nonempty interior, the formalization proves
> $\inf_{z\in\mathrm{int}\,K}|K|\,|(K-z)^\circ|\ge (n+1)^{n+1}/(n!)^2$.
> Equality holds exactly when $K$ is a simplex. The paper's functional inequality is outside this selected statement.
>
> For an origin-symmetric convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$, $n\ge2$, the formalized result determines the symplectic ball capacity of $\mathrm{int}\,K\times\mathrm{int}\,K^\circ$: its Gromov width is $4$, and every ball of capacity $0<c<4$ embeds symplectically into it. The normalization assigns capacity $\pi r^2$ to a ball of radius $r$.
>
> No boundary smoothness or strict convexity is assumed. An embedding at capacity exactly $4$ is not asserted.

Comparator source `GeneralMahler.lean` (1238 bytes, sha256 `4c2ff125052477b16e8ba2e85e641e65fc6790e08da7990fa309a5e9033f9364`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section

open Set MeasureTheory
open scoped RealInnerProductSpace

namespace GeneralMahler

abbrev Rn (n : ℕ) := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin n)

/-- The polar of a convex body translated by an interior center. -/
def polarAt {n : ℕ} (K : Set (Rn n)) (z : Rn n) : Set (Rn n) :=
  {y | ∀ x ∈ K, ⟪y, x - z⟫ ≤ 1}

/-- The volume product minimized over the interior centers of the body. -/
def volumeProduct {n : ℕ} (K : Set (EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin n))) : ℝ :=
  sInf ((fun z => (volume K).toReal * (volume (polarAt K z)).toReal) ''
    interior K)

/-- The sharp general Mahler inequality, with equality exactly for simplices. -/
theorem general_mahler {n : ℕ} (hn : 1 ≤ n)
    (K : Set (EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin n)))
    (hcompact : IsCompact K) (hconvex : Convex ℝ K)
    (hinterior : (interior K).Nonempty) :
    ((n : ℝ) + 1) ^ (n + 1) / (Nat.factorial n : ℝ) ^ 2 ≤ volumeProduct K ∧
      (volumeProduct K = ((n : ℝ) + 1) ^ (n + 1) / (Nat.factorial n : ℝ) ^ 2 ↔
        ∃ vertices : Fin (n + 1) → EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin n),
          AffineIndependent ℝ vertices ∧ K = convexHull ℝ (range vertices)) := by
  sorry

end GeneralMahler

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We resolve the symmetric Mahler conjecture positively, including its equality classification. Every origin-symmetric convex body in ℝ<sup><i>n</i></sup> has volume product at least $`4^n/n!`$, with equality exactly for invertible linear images of Hanner polytopes.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

A convex body is a compact convex subset of $\mathbb R^n$ with nonempty interior. If $K=-K$, its center $0$ is an interior point, and its polar body is $$K^\circ=\{y\in\mathbb R^n:\langle x,y\rangle\le1
                         \text{ for every }x\in K\}.$$ Write $|K|$ for $n$-dimensional Lebesgue volume and $P(K)=|K|\,|K^\circ|$ for the volume product. The two determinant factors cancel under an invertible linear change of coordinates, so $P(TK)=P(K)$. Mahler’s symmetric volume-product problem asks how small this linear invariant can be. The conjectured value is $4^n/n!$, the product of a cube and its polar cross-polytope. We prove this bound in every dimension and classify the equality cases.

The extremal bodies are defined recursively. A centered nondegenerate interval is a one-dimensional *Hanner polytope*. Given Hanner polytopes $H_1\subset\mathbb R^k$ and $H_2\subset\mathbb R^l$, their Cartesian product and the convex hull $$\begin{aligned}
 H_1\oplus_\infty H_2&=H_1\times H_2,\\
 H_1\oplus_1 H_2&=
 \mathop{\mathrm{conv}}\bigl((H_1\times\{0\})\cup(\{0\}\times H_2)\bigr)
 \end{aligned}$$ are Hanner polytopes in $\mathbb R^{k+l}$. These are the $\ell_\infty$ and $\ell_1$ sums, respectively. This class contains cubes, cross-polytopes, and mixed recursive sums. We call an invertible linear image of a Hanner polytope a *linear Hanner body*.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For every integer $n\ge1$ and every origin-symmetric convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$, <a id="eq:main-inequality"></a>$$\label{eq:main-inequality}
 |K|\,|K^\circ|\ge\frac{4^n}{n!}.$$ Equality holds if and only if $K$ is a linear Hanner body.*

</div>

The linear equivalence in the statement respects the fixed origin used for polarity.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### History and context

Mahler introduced the volume-product problem in his work on convex bodies and transference in the geometry of numbers (Mahler 1939) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Mahler1939](#ref-Mahler1939)]; the planar inequality also goes back to him (Mahler 1938) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[MahlerPlanar1939](#ref-MahlerPlanar1939)]. Reisner later characterized its equality cases as parallelograms, as a consequence of his zonoid theorem (Reisner 1986, 340) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Reisner1986](#ref-Reisner1986)]. Iriyeh and Shibata proved the complete three-dimensional symmetric theorem, including equality (Iriyeh and Shibata 2020, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[IriyehShibata](#ref-IriyehShibata)]. Fradelizi, Hubard, Meyer, Roldán-Pensado, and Zvavitch gave a shorter proof based on equipartitions, together with another equality argument and stability (Fradelizi et al. 2022) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[FradeliziHubardMeyerRoldanZvavitch2022](#ref-FradeliziHubardMeyerRoldanZvavitch2022)]. Chen, Li, Xi, and Xu recently gave a shadow-flow proof of that theorem in a preprint that also treats the nonsymmetric three-dimensional problem (Chen et al. 2026, Theorem 7.1) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[ChenLiXiXu2026](#ref-ChenLiXiXu2026)].

Hanner’s recursively constructed spaces (Hanner 1956) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Hanner1956](#ref-Hanner1956)] already play a central role in the known higher-dimensional cases. Saint-Raymond proved the sharp inequality for unconditional bodies (those invariant under coordinate sign changes) (Saint-Raymond 1981) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[SaintRaymond1981](#ref-SaintRaymond1981)]; Meyer and Reisner identified the Hanner equality cases in that class (Meyer 1986; Reisner 1987) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Meyer1986](#ref-Meyer1986), [Reisner1987](#ref-Reisner1987)]. Reisner also proved the sharp inequality for zonoids, which are Hausdorff limits of Minkowski sums of centered segments, and showed that equality within that class means a parallelotope (Reisner 1985, 1986) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Reisner1985](#ref-Reisner1985), [Reisner1986](#ref-Reisner1986)]; Gordon, Meyer, and Reisner later gave a short geometric proof (Gordon, Meyer, and Reisner 1988) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[GordonMeyerReisner1988](#ref-GordonMeyerReisner1988)]. For local questions in Banach–Mazur distance, Nazarov, Petrov, Ryabogin, and Zvavitch established strict local minimality, up to linear equivalence, of the cube (Nazarov et al. 2010) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[NazarovPetrovRyaboginZvavitch2010](#ref-NazarovPetrovRyaboginZvavitch2010)]. Kim extended this to all Hanner polytopes (Kim 2014) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Kim2014](#ref-Kim2014)]. Kim and Zvavitch obtained stability in the unconditional class and the inequality in a neighborhood of that class (Kim and Zvavitch 2015) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[KimZvavitch2015](#ref-KimZvavitch2015)].

A separate line of work seeks dimension-independent exponential bounds without the sharp constant. Bourgain and Milman established such a reverse Blaschke–Santaló inequality (Bourgain and Milman 1987) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[BourgainMilman](#ref-BourgainMilman)]; Kuperberg obtained an explicit stronger bound by a geometric argument using Gauss linking integrals (Kuperberg 2008) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Kuperberg](#ref-Kuperberg)]. Complex-analytic approaches provide another setting for these questions. Nazarov used Hörmander’s $\bar\partial$ theorem and Bergman kernels for the reverse Blaschke–Santaló inequality (Nazarov 2012) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Nazarov2012](#ref-Nazarov2012)]. Berndtsson recast Kuperberg’s proof using complex integrals (Berndtsson 2021) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Berndtsson](#ref-Berndtsson)]. In a symplectic approach, Karasev proved the sharp inequality for hyperplane sections and projections of $\ell_p$ balls ($1\le p\le\infty$) and Hanner polytopes (Karasev 2021, Theorems 4.2 and 4.4) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Karasev](#ref-Karasev)]. These works give analytic and geometric precedents for volume-product estimates; their estimates are not hypotheses of our proof.

The analytic ingredient is a conformal map of the disk for which uniform angular measure on the boundary maps to a uniform imaginary coordinate. It is a rotation of Gross’s example for the uniform distribution in his conformal Skorokhod embedding (Gross 2019, sec. 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Gross](#ref-Gross)]. A holomorphic mass estimate turns that boundary law into a lower bound for an integral of feasible-simplex volumes over the primal body. We prove the mass estimate directly by Stokes’ theorem, with its precise normalization; its general background is the theory of generalized Lelong numbers (Demailly 1993, sec. 3 and 5) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Demailly](#ref-Demailly)]. The symmetric-convex extremal-function framework of Lundin and Baran was a further motivation for the analytic search; see (Lundin 1985) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Lundin1985](#ref-Lundin1985)] and the formula recorded in (Bos, Calvi, and Levenberg 2001, Proposition 1.2, pp. 246–247) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[BosCalviLevenberg2001](#ref-BosCalviLevenberg2001)]. The proof here instead uses the direct isolated-zero mass calculation, not those extremal-function formulas as premises. The equality argument leads to a classical property of normed spaces: any three pairwise-intersecting closed balls have a common point. Hanner studied the associated recursively constructed convex bodies (Hanner 1956) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Hanner1956](#ref-Hanner1956)]. Lima’s norm-additive decomposition criterion (Lima 1978, Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Lima1978](#ref-Lima1978)], also recorded in Hansen–Lima (Hansen and Lima 1981, Theorem 3.6(4)) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[HansenLima1981](#ref-HansenLima1981)], is exactly the metric-median condition used below. Reisner’s unconditional equality theorem already connects minimal volume product to this ball intersection structure (Reisner 1987, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Reisner1987](#ref-Reisner1987)]. Our endpoint argument obtains the structure without assuming unconditionality; Hansen–Lima’s finite-dimensional classification is the final input (Hansen and Lima 1981, Corollary 7.4) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[HansenLima1981](#ref-HansenLima1981)].

For a body without a prescribed center, the general Mahler problem instead minimizes $|K|\,|(K-z)^\circ|$ over $z\in\operatorname{int}K$. Its conjectured constant $(n+1)^{n+1}/(n!)^2$ is smaller than the symmetric constant for $n\ge2$; a result for that general problem alone therefore does not establish Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. The argument here uses symmetry throughout and is independent of the general theorem proved in the companion paper (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[OpenAIGeneralMahler2026](#ref-OpenAIGeneralMahler2026)].

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Proof overview

Section <a href="#sec:convex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:convex">2</a> records polarity and the two norm-sum formulas. They show that every Hanner body has the stated volume product. The remaining work is the lower bound and its converse equality statement.

First let $$A=\{X\in\mathbb R^d:|b_i\cdot X|\le1,\ 1\le i\le m\},
 \qquad A^\circ=\mathop{\mathrm{conv}}\{\pm b_1,\ldots,\pm b_m\},$$ where the nonzero, pairwise nonproportional rows $b_i$ span $\mathbb R^d$. Section <a href="#sec:lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:lens">3</a> constructs a planar lens with horizontal half-width $\lambda(t)$ at height $t$. For $X\in\operatorname{int}A$, use this width to form $$L_X=\{Y\in\mathbb R^d:|b_i\cdot Y|\le\lambda(b_i\cdot X),\ 1\le i\le m\}.$$ Each independent $d$-tuple of signed constraints that are tight at one point of $L_X$ determines a simplex: take the convex hull of $0$ and those signed rows. Let $\Sigma_X$ be the union of these simplices. It lies in $A^\circ$.

Write $F$ for the conformal map from the unit disk to the lens. For each set $I$ of $d$ independent rows, sample independent uniform angles $\theta_i$ and solve $b_iZ=F(e^{i\theta_i})$, $i\in I$, for the complex vector $Z$. Let $P_I$ be the probability that all remaining row coordinates also lie in the closed lens. Section <a href="#sec:feasibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:feasibility">5</a> applies the mass estimate of Section <a href="#sec:holomorphic-mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:holomorphic-mass">4</a> to high powers of the inverse map: after a change of radial variables, the Jacobian integrals concentrate on these boundary samples and force $S:=\sum_I P_I\ge1$. The uniform imaginary coordinate then identifies this probability sum with the simplex integral in Section <a href="#sec:simplex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:simplex">6</a>: $$1\le S=\frac{d!}{4^d}\int_A|\Sigma_X|\,dX
                    \le\frac{d!}{4^d}|A|\,|A^\circ|.$$ The first inequality is analytic; the last is the inclusion just described. The exact identity behind this bound also controls the missing volume $|A^\circ|-|\Sigma_X|$. Approximation extends the inequality to all origin-symmetric convex bodies.

For equality, set $Q=K^\circ$ and pass to the body $$B=K\oplus_1[-1,1],\qquad B^\circ=Q\times[-1,1]$$ in dimension $d=n+1$. The norm-sum formula preserves equality at the new dimension. Inner polytope approximations to $Q$ therefore have integrated missing volume tending to zero. Section <a href="#sec:zero-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:zero-deficit">7</a> uses product neighborhoods to obtain, for each interior point of $B$ and each nonzero direction, a representation whose rows attain their lens bounds at one vector satisfying all the constraints. This includes degenerate limiting representations. Finally, Section <a href="#sec:median" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:median">8</a> examines points approaching an apex of $B$. The lens asymptotic gives a support-function inequality, and convex separation gives a metric median for every triple in the norm with unit ball $Q$: a point whose distances to each pair sum to that pair’s distance. The Hansen–Lima theorem then identifies $Q$, and hence $K$, as a linear Hanner body (Hansen and Lima 1981, Corollary 7.4) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[HansenLima1981](#ref-HansenLima1981)].

We next derive functional and entropy–transport consequences of the all-dimensional theorem. The geometric proof begins in Section <a href="#sec:convex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:convex">2</a> and ends in Section <a href="#sec:median" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:median">8</a>.

<a id="sec:functional-mahler"></a>
#### Functional Mahler inequality

The volume-product problem has a functional form in which polarity is replaced by convex conjugation. For a proper lower-semicontinuous convex function $\varphi:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R\cup\{+\infty\}$, its Fenchel–Legendre transform is $$\varphi^*(y)=\sup_{x\in\mathbb R^n}\{\langle x,y\rangle-\varphi(x)\},
 \qquad y\in\mathbb R^n.$$ We use the convention $e^{-\infty}=0$.

<a id="stmt-cor-functional-mahler"></a>
<div id="cor:functional-mahler" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2** (Even functional Mahler inequality). *For every integer $n\ge1$ and every even proper lower-semicontinuous convex function $\varphi:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R\cup\{+\infty\}$ such that $0<\int_{\mathbb R^n}e^{-\varphi(x)}\,dx<\infty$, $$\left(\int_{\mathbb R^n}e^{-\varphi(x)}\,dx\right)
 \left(\int_{\mathbb R^n}e^{-\varphi^*(y)}\,dy\right)\ge4^n.$$ The second integral is allowed to be infinite, in which case the inequality is immediate.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-functional-mahler"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-functional-mahler</summary>

*Proof.* Set $f=e^{-\varphi}$, a nonzero integrable log-concave function. For $z\in\mathbb R^n$, Fradelizi and Meyer use the polar function $$f^z(y)=\inf_{\{x:f(x)>0\}}
        \frac{e^{-\langle x-z,y-z\rangle}}{f(x)}$$ and the translation-minimized functional volume product $$\mathcal P(f)=
 \left(\int_{\mathbb R^n}f(x)\,dx\right)
 \inf_{z\in\mathbb R^n}\int_{\mathbb R^n}f^z(y)\,dy.$$ Their Proposition 1(a) states that the symmetric geometric Mahler inequality in every dimension implies $\mathcal P(f)\ge4^n$ for every even log-concave function on $\mathbb R^n$ with $0<\int_{\mathbb R^n}f<\infty$ (Fradelizi and Meyer 2008) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[FradeliziMeyer2008](#ref-FradeliziMeyer2008)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> supplies this all-dimensional hypothesis. Since $f^0=e^{-\varphi^*}$, the product in Corollary <a href="#cor:functional-mahler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:functional-mahler">2</a> is at least $\mathcal P(f)$. ◻

</details>

The cited implication uses auxiliary convex bodies in dimensions $n+m$ and lets $m$ tend to infinity; it is not an application only of the geometric theorem in dimension $n$. The constant is sharp: for $\varphi(x)=\sum_{i=1}^n|x_i|$, the transform $\varphi^*$ is zero on $[-1,1]^n$ and $+\infty$ outside, and both integrals in the corollary equal $2^n$. The equality classification in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> concerns convex bodies. The limiting implication does not by itself classify the even potentials for which equality holds (Fradelizi and Meyer 2008, 1437) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[FradeliziMeyer2008](#ref-FradeliziMeyer2008)].

For potentials without evenness, the companion paper on general convex bodies gives the sharp lower bound $e^n$ (OpenAI 2026, Corollary 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[OpenAIGeneralMahler2026](#ref-OpenAIGeneralMahler2026)].

The functional inequality also has an entropy–transport formulation. For a log-concave probability measure $\eta$ with a density, let $H(\eta)=\int\log(d\eta/dx)\,d\eta$ denote its entropy relative to Lebesgue measure, the negative of differential Shannon entropy. For probability measures $\mu_1,\mu_2$ with finite first moments, set $$\mathcal T(\mu_1,\mu_2)=
 \inf_{f\in\mathcal F}\left\{\int f\,d\mu_1+\int f^*\,d\mu_2\right\},$$ where $\mathcal F$ is the class of proper lower-semicontinuous convex functions $\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R\cup\{+\infty\}$ and $f^*$ is the Fenchel–Legendre transform. The cost $\mathcal T$ is allowed to be $+\infty$.

<a id="stmt-cor-entropy-transport"></a>
<div id="cor:entropy-transport" class="corollary">

**Corollary 3** (Symmetric entropy–transport inequality). *For every integer $n\ge1$, let $\eta_i(dx)=e^{-V_i(x)}\,dx$, $i=1,2$, be full-dimensional log-concave probability measures, both symmetric about the origin, with proper lower-semicontinuous convex potentials $V_i$. Suppose their densities are essentially continuous, meaning that $e^{-V_i}=0$ at $\mathcal H^{n-1}$-almost every point of $\partial\operatorname{supp}\eta_i$. Their moment measures $\nu_i=(\nabla V_i)_\#\eta_i$ have finite first moments, $H(\eta_i)=-\int V_i\,d\eta_i$ is finite, and $$H(\eta_1)+H(\eta_2)\le -n\log(4e^2)+\mathcal T(\nu_1,\nu_2).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-entropy-transport"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-entropy-transport</summary>

*Proof.* For $\rho_i=e^{-V_i}$, the identity $|\nabla\rho_i|=|\nabla V_i|e^{-V_i}$ holds almost everywhere on the interior of the support. Lemma 4 and the proof of Proposition 7 of (Cordero-Erausquin and Klartag 2015) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[CorderoErausquinKlartag2015](#ref-CorderoErausquinKlartag2015)] give $\int |\nabla\rho_i|<\infty$ and $V_i\in L^1(\eta_i)$, respectively. The pushforward definition of $\nu_i$ therefore gives its finite first moment, and $H(\eta_i)=-\int V_i\,d\eta_i$ is finite. Gozlan’s equivalence between the symmetric functional inverse Santaló and entropy–transport inequalities, as stated in (Fradelizi, Gozlan, and Zugmeyer 2021, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[FradeliziGozlanZugmeyer2021](#ref-FradeliziGozlanZugmeyer2021)], applies to Corollary <a href="#cor:functional-mahler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:functional-mahler">2</a> with $c=4$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:convex"></a>
### Polarity, norm sums, and Hanner polytopes

<a id="eq:gauge-support"></a>
<a id="eq:linear-polarity"></a>
All bodies below are symmetric about the origin. In addition to the polar and volume product defined in Section <a href="#sec:introduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:introduction">1</a>, we use the gauge and support function $$p_A(x)=\inf\{t>0:x\in tA\},\qquad
 h_A(y)=\max_{x\in A}\langle x,y\rangle.$$ Dual spaces are identified with Euclidean coordinate spaces, and $|A|$ always denotes volume in the dimension of $A$. The notation $h_A$ also applies to any nonempty compact convex set. The gauge is a norm with closed unit ball $A$. Convex separation gives the bipolar identity $A^{\circ\circ}=A$, and hence $$\label{eq:gauge-support}
 p_A=h_{A^\circ},\qquad h_A=p_{A^\circ}.$$ Indeed $x\in tA$ is equivalent to $\langle x,y\rangle\le t$ for every $y\in A^\circ$. For an invertible linear map $T$, $$\label{eq:linear-polarity}
 (TA)^\circ=T^{-\mathsf T}A^\circ,
 \qquad P(TA)=P(A),$$ since the two volumes acquire reciprocal determinant factors.

<a id="eq:sum-gauges"></a>
For bodies $A\subset\mathbb R^k$ and $A'\subset\mathbb R^l$, define $$A\oplus_\infty A'=A\times A',\qquad
 A\oplus_1 A'=\operatorname{conv}
       \bigl((A\times\{0\})\cup(\{0\}\times A')\bigr).$$ These operations are taken in the product space. More generally, complementary subspaces are identified with product coordinates by an invertible linear map. Their gauges are $$\label{eq:sum-gauges}
 \begin{split}
 p_{A\oplus_\infty A'}(x,x')&=\max\{p_A(x),p_{A'}(x')\},\\
 p_{A\oplus_1 A'}(x,x')&=p_A(x)+p_{A'}(x').
 \end{split}$$ For the second identity, a convex combination from the two coordinate bodies has gauge sum at most one. Conversely, when the sum is at most one, use these two gauges as the coefficients of the normalized coordinate vectors and place any remaining weight at $0$. A zero coordinate contributes no term.

<a id="stmt-lem-sums"></a>
<div id="lem:sums" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:sum-products"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Polars and volumes of the two sums). *For origin-symmetric convex bodies of dimensions $k,l\ge1$, <a id="eq:sum-polars"></a>$$\label{eq:sum-polars}
 (A\oplus_1 A')^\circ=A^\circ\oplus_\infty(A')^\circ,
 \qquad
 (A\oplus_\infty A')^\circ=A^\circ\oplus_1(A')^\circ.$$ Moreover, <a id="eq:sum-volumes"></a>$$\label{eq:sum-volumes}
 |A\oplus_\infty A'|=|A|\,|A'|,
 \qquad
 |A\oplus_1 A'|=\frac{k!\,l!}{(k+l)!}|A|\,|A'|.$$ Consequently, for $s\in\{1,\infty\}$, $$\label{eq:sum-products}
 P(A\oplus_s A')=\frac{P(A)P(A')}{\binom{k+l}{k}}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-sums"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-sums</summary>

*Proof.* Testing a functional on the two coordinate bodies gives the first polar identity; taking polars again gives the second. The product volume follows from Fubini. By <a href="#eq:sum-gauges" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sum-gauges">[eq:sum-gauges]</a>, the fiber of $A\oplus_1 A'$ over $x\in A$ is $(1-p_A(x))A'$. Since $|\{x:p_A(x)\le t\}|=t^k|A|$ for $0\le t\le1$, integration gives $$|A\oplus_1 A'|
 =k|A|\,|A'|\int_0^1t^{k-1}(1-t)^l\,dt
 =\frac{k!\,l!}{(k+l)!}|A|\,|A'|.$$ The last integral is evaluated by repeated integration by parts. Combining the polar and volume identities proves <a href="#eq:sum-products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sum-products">[eq:sum-products]</a>. ◻

</details>

The Hanner recursion from Section <a href="#sec:introduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:introduction">1</a> uses precisely these two operations, following Hanner (Hanner 1956) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[Hanner1956](#ref-Hanner1956)]. Their volume and polarity formulas give the attaining family immediately.

<a id="stmt-lem-hanner"></a>
<div id="lem:hanner" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (The Hanner volume product). *Every $d$-dimensional linear Hanner body $H$ satisfies $$P(H)=\frac{4^d}{d!}.$$ The polar of a linear Hanner body is again a linear Hanner body.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hanner"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hanner</summary>

*Proof.* A centered interval $[-a,a]$, $a>0$, has polar $[-1/a,1/a]$ and volume product $4$. Equation <a href="#eq:sum-products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sum-products">[eq:sum-products]</a> propagates $4^k/k!$ and $4^l/l!$ to $4^{k+l}/(k+l)!$ under either operation. Induction and linear invariance prove the first assertion. The polar identities exchange the two operations and preserve the interval base case. Equation <a href="#eq:linear-polarity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:linear-polarity">[eq:linear-polarity]</a> then proves closure under polarity also for linear images. ◻

</details>

In dimension one every origin-symmetric convex body is a centered nondegenerate interval. Thus both the sharp inequality and the full equality classification in that dimension follow directly from the base case above.

<a id="sec:lens"></a>
### A conformal lens with a uniform boundary coordinate

We construct a conformal image of the disk whose boundary has a uniformly distributed imaginary coordinate. This law will turn complex feasibility probabilities into real volumes. The horizontal half-width has further roles: strict curvature will make additional boundary equalities have probability zero, while concavity will extend feasibility from polar vertices to their convex hull. Its endpoint scaling will enter the equality argument near the apex of the lifted body. The map is a rotation of the uniform-distribution example in Gross’s conformal Skorokhod embedding (Gross 2019, sec. 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[Gross](#ref-Gross)]; we prove all its required properties directly.

Write $\mathbb D=\{z\in\mathbb C:|z|<1\}$ and define <a id="eq:lens-map"></a>$$\label{eq:lens-map}
 F(z)=\frac8{\pi^2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}
             \frac{(-1)^jz^{2j+1}}{(2j+1)^2}.$$ The series converges uniformly on $\overline{\mathbb D}$.

<a id="stmt-lem-planar-lens"></a>
<div id="lem:planar-lens" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:lens-domain"></a>
<a id="eq:lens-width-curvature"></a>
<a id="eq:lens-boundary-law"></a>
**Lemma 6** (The planar lens). *The function $F$ is a biholomorphism from $\mathbb D$ onto a bounded convex domain $D$, with inverse $g$, and $F'(0)=8/\pi^2$. There is a continuous even function $\lambda:[-1,1]\to[0,\infty)$, positive on $(-1,1)$ and zero at $\pm1$, such that $$\label{eq:lens-domain}
 \overline D=\{v+it:|t|\leq1,\ |v|\leq\lambda(t)\}.$$ The function $\lambda$ is smooth on $(-1,1)$ and satisfies $$\label{eq:lens-width-curvature}
 \lambda'(t)=-\frac2\pi
       \operatorname{arctanh}\!\left(\sin\frac{\pi t}{2}\right),
 \qquad
 \lambda''(t)=-\sec\frac{\pi t}{2}<0.$$ If $\Theta$ is uniform on $[0,2\pi)$, then $$\label{eq:lens-boundary-law}
 F(e^{i\Theta})\ \stackrel{\mathrm{law}}{=}\
 \varepsilon\lambda(T)+iT,$$ where $T$ is uniform on $[-1,1]$ and $\varepsilon$ is an independent uniform sign.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-planar-lens"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-planar-lens</summary>

*Proof.* The map is odd and commutes with conjugation. Put $$w=\arctan z=\frac1{2i}\log\frac{1+iz}{1-iz},\qquad z\in\mathbb D.$$ The fraction inside the logarithm lies in the right half-plane, where we use the principal logarithm. Thus $|\Re w|<\pi/4$, $\tan w=z$, and $w=0$ only when $z=0$. Differentiating the series gives <a id="eq:lens-derivatives"></a>$$\label{eq:lens-derivatives}
 F'(z)=\frac8{\pi^2}\frac wz,\qquad
 1+\frac{zF''(z)}{F'(z)}=\frac{\sin(2w)}{2w},$$ with their limiting values at zero. In particular $F'$ has no zeros. For $w=a+ib\ne0$, the real part of the last expression is $$\frac{a\sin(2a)\cosh(2b)+b\cos(2a)\sinh(2b)}{2(a^2+b^2)}>0,$$ since $|a|<\pi/4$; its value at zero is $1$.

Fix $0<r<1$ and put $\gamma_r(\theta)=F(re^{i\theta})$. This curve is regular, and its tangent angle $\beta$ satisfies $$\beta'(\theta)=\Re\!\left(1+\frac{zF''(z)}{F'(z)}\right)>0,
 \qquad \beta(\theta+2\pi)=\beta(\theta)+2\pi.$$ The second identity uses the zero-free derivative in the disk. At a fixed parameter $\theta_0$, project the curve onto its right unit normal there. The derivative of this projection has the sign of $-\sin(\beta(\theta)-\beta(\theta_0))$. It is negative until the tangent has turned through $\pi$, and positive thereafter, until the curve returns to $\theta_0$. Hence the projection has its unique maximum at $\theta_0$. This proves simplicity and shows that every point of the curve is the unique supporting point of its convex hull in the corresponding normal direction. All normal directions occur, so the curve is the boundary of that convex hull and is positively oriented. The harmonic maximum principle for these supporting projections places $F(r\mathbb D)$ strictly inside the curve. The argument principle then gives exactly one preimage for every point inside and none outside. Thus $F$ maps $r\mathbb D$ biholomorphically onto its bounded convex interior $D_r$.

As $r$ increases, these domains exhaust $D=F(\mathbb D)$. Consequently $F$ is univalent on $\mathbb D$, $D$ is convex, and its inverse $g$ is holomorphic. For each $0<r<1$, continuity also gives $\overline{D_r}=F(r\overline{\mathbb D})\subset D$. Continuity on the closed disk gives boundedness and $\overline D=F(\overline{\mathbb D})$. No boundary value $F(\zeta)$, $|\zeta|=1$, lies in $D$: otherwise continuity of $g$ there and $g(F(r\zeta))=r\zeta$ would give $g(F(\zeta))=\zeta\notin\mathbb D$. It follows that $\partial D=F(\partial\mathbb D)$.

We now compute this boundary. On the right semicircle, $z=e^{is}$ with $-\pi/2<s<\pi/2$, one has $$\frac{1+iz}{1-iz}=\frac{i\cos s}{1+\sin s},\qquad
 \Re w=\frac\pi4,\qquad
 \Im w=\frac12\operatorname{arctanh}(\sin s).$$ The formulas for $w$ and $F'$ extend analytically across each compact subarc, so we may differentiate the boundary values. Writing $F(e^{is})=v(s)+it(s)$ gives $$t'(s)=\frac2\pi,\qquad
 v'(s)=-\frac4{\pi^2}\operatorname{arctanh}(\sin s).$$ Since $t(0)=0$, we have $t(s)=2s/\pi$. Define $\lambda(t)=v(\pi t/2)$ and extend it to $[-1,1]$ by continuity. Conjugation makes $\lambda$ even. The values $F(\pm i)$ are purely imaginary by the series, so $\lambda(\pm1)=0$. Differentiation gives <a href="#eq:lens-width-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lens-width-curvature">[eq:lens-width-curvature]</a>; strict concavity and the endpoint values then give positivity in the interior. Oddness and conjugation show that the other semicircle traces the graph $v=-\lambda(t)$. These two graphs give <a href="#eq:lens-domain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lens-domain">[eq:lens-domain]</a>. On either semicircle the imaginary coordinate traverses $[-1,1]$ at constant absolute speed $2/\pi$. Each semicircle has probability $1/2$, which proves <a href="#eq:lens-boundary-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lens-boundary-law">[eq:lens-boundary-law]</a>. ◻

</details>

Figure <a href="#fig:lens-boundary-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:lens-boundary-law">1</a> illustrates the coordinate law.

<figure id="fig:lens-boundary-law">
<embed src="figures/lens-boundary-law.pdf" />
<figcaption>The conformal lens and its boundary coordinate. Equally spaced angles on either semicircle give equally spaced imaginary coordinates on the corresponding boundary branch. On the right semicircle, $F(e^{is}) = λ(2s/π) + 2is/π$; reflection gives the left branch. Thus a uniform angle gives a uniform coordinate in $[−1,1]$ and an independent fair branch sign. The horizontal arrow marks the half-width $λ(t_{0})$, with $s_{0} = π/6$ and $t_{0} = 1/3$.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-lens-boundary-law"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:lens-boundary-law`)

The source figure is an external graphic; no image payload is embedded.

Visual witness description of PDF page 9 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Two diagrams side by side: (a) labelled "(a) Equal angular steps" showing the unit disk $\mathbb{D}$ in the complex plane with axes $\Re z$ and $+\Im z$, an angle $s_0$ from the real axis, and point $e^{i s_0}$ on the right semicircle; a horizontal arrow labelled $F$ with $t_0 = 2s_0/\pi$ points from (a) to (b); (b) labelled "(b) Equal vertical steps" showing the conformal lens domain $D$ with vertical axis $t$ (marked at $1$, $t_0$, $-1$) and horizontal axis $v$, with a horizontal arrow from the vertical axis at $t_0$ to the right boundary curve labelled $\lambda(t_0)$. Centered below both diagrams is the formula $F(e^{is}) = \lambda(2s/\pi) + i 2s/\pi \quad (-\pi/2 < s < \pi/2)$.
```


The two boundary graphs are smooth away from $\pm i$ and have strictly turning tangent directions by <a href="#eq:lens-width-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lens-width-curvature">[eq:lens-width-curvature]</a>. In particular, a fixed tangent line direction occurs at only finitely many parameters away from these two endpoints. The boundary has planar Lebesgue measure zero, as is also immediate from its graph description.

<a id="stmt-lem-lens-endpoint"></a>
<div id="lem:lens-endpoint" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:lens-endpoint-asymptotic"></a>
<a id="eq:lens-endpoint-ratio"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Endpoint scaling). *As $\eta\downarrow0$, $$\label{eq:lens-endpoint-asymptotic}
 \lambda(1-\eta)=\frac2\pi\eta\log\frac1\eta+O(\eta).$$ For every $0<a\leq b<\infty$, $$\label{eq:lens-endpoint-ratio}
 \sup_{a\leq c\leq b}
 \left|\frac{\lambda(1-\delta c)}{\lambda(1-\delta)}-c\right|
 \longrightarrow0\qquad(\delta\downarrow0).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-lens-endpoint"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-lens-endpoint</summary>

*Proof.* Equation <a href="#eq:lens-width-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lens-width-curvature">[eq:lens-width-curvature]</a> and $\operatorname{arctanh}(\cos x)=\log\cot(x/2)$ give $$\lambda(1-\eta)=\frac2\pi\int_0^\eta
                  \log\cot\frac{\pi s}{4}\,ds.$$ Here $\log\cot(\pi s/4)=\log(1/s)+O(1)$ uniformly as $s\downarrow0$. Integration proves <a href="#eq:lens-endpoint-asymptotic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lens-endpoint-asymptotic">[eq:lens-endpoint-asymptotic]</a>. Writing $L=\log(1/\delta)$, the same uniform remainder gives $$\frac{\lambda(1-\delta c)}{\lambda(1-\delta)}
 =c\,\frac{L-\log c+O(1)}{L+O(1)}
 =c+O_{a,b}(L^{-1})$$ uniformly for $a\leq c\leq b$, which proves <a href="#eq:lens-endpoint-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lens-endpoint-ratio">[eq:lens-endpoint-ratio]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:holomorphic-mass"></a>
### Mass at an isolated holomorphic zero

The next estimate turns the order of a holomorphic zero into an integral bound for its Jacobian minors. Its application will use high powers of the inverse planar map. In complex dimension $d$, the mass bound grows as the $d$th power of their exponent, matching the radial rescaling that will produce boundary feasibility probabilities in Section <a href="#sec:feasibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:feasibility">5</a>. The estimate is a special case of the sublevel-mass and comparison formulas for generalized Lelong numbers; see Demailly (Demailly 1993, sec. 3 and 5) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[Demailly](#ref-Demailly)]. We give a direct Stokes proof in the normalization used here.

<a id="eq:mass-dc-convention"></a>
<a id="eq:mass-volume-normalization"></a>
On $\mathbb C^d$, write $z_j=x_j+iy_j$ and use the complex orientation $$dV=dx_1\wedge dy_1\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_d\wedge dy_d.$$ For a smooth real function $v$, our convention is $$\label{eq:mass-dc-convention}
d^c=\frac{i}{4}(\bar\partial-\partial),
\qquad dd^c=\frac{i}{2}\partial\bar\partial.$$ Thus $d^cv(\xi)=-\tfrac14dv(i\xi)$ for a real tangent vector $\xi$, and $$\label{eq:mass-volume-normalization}
dd^c|z|^2=\sum_{j=1}^d dx_j\wedge dy_j,
\qquad
(dd^cv)^d=d!\det(v_{\bar z_i z_j})\,dV.$$ The second identity follows by expanding the exterior product, or by a unitary diagonalization of the Hermitian Hessian.

<a id="stmt-lem-holomorphic-mass"></a>
<div id="lem:holomorphic-mass" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Holomorphic mass). *Let $d\geq1$, $N\geq d$, and let $\mathcal U\subset\mathbb C^d$ be open and contain $0$. Suppose that $f=(f_1,\ldots,f_N):\mathcal U\to\mathbb C^N$ is holomorphic and has the following properties:*

1.  *$f^{-1}(0)=\{0\}$;*

2.  *for an integer $k\geq1$, $$f(z)=H(z)+O(|z|^{k+1})\quad\text{near }0,$$ where each component of $H$ is a homogeneous polynomial of degree $k$ and $H(z)\ne0$ whenever $z\ne0$;*

3.  *for every $0<s<1$, the set $\{z\in\mathcal U:|f(z)|^2\leq s\}$ is compact in $\mathcal U$.*

<a id="eq:holomorphic-minor-mass"></a>
*Then, with $\tau=|f|^2$, $$\label{eq:holomorphic-minor-mass}
\int_{\{\tau<1\}}
 \sum_{\substack{I\subset\{1,\ldots,N\}\\|I|=d}}
 \left|\det\left(\frac{\partial f_i}{\partial z_j}\right)_{
                       i\in I,\ 1\leq j\leq d}\right|^2\,dV
\geq \frac{(\pi k)^d}{d!}.$$ All norms are Euclidean. Rows in each determinant are in increasing index order.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-holomorphic-mass"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-holomorphic-mass</summary>

*Proof.* We first bound the integral of $(dd^c\tau)^d$. The conversion to Jacobian minors will then follow from Cauchy–Binet. Both $\tau$ and $\log\tau$ away from $0$ have positive semidefinite complex Hessians. Indeed, if $Z$ is the complex derivative matrix of $f$, the Hessian of $\tau$ is $Z^*Z$. For a complex direction $\xi$, the Hessian of $\log\tau$ evaluates to $$\frac{|f|^2|df(\xi)|^2
       -|\langle df(\xi),f\rangle|^2}{|f|^4}\geq0$$ by Cauchy–Schwarz. Their top exterior powers are therefore nonnegative.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
###### Comparison with a small sphere.

Fix a regular value $s\in(0,1)$ of $\tau$ and set $E_s=\{z\in\mathcal U:\tau(z)<s\}$. By compactness of the sublevel and the regular-value theorem, $\overline{E_s}$ is a compact smooth manifold with boundary $\{\tau=s\}$. Stokes’ theorem gives $$\int_{E_s}(dd^c\tau)^d
 =\int_{\partial E_s}d^c\tau\wedge(dd^c\tau)^{d-1}.$$ Put $\gamma=d^c\log\tau$ on $\mathcal U\setminus\{0\}$. The identities $$d^c\log\tau=\tau^{-1}d^c\tau,
\qquad
dd^c\log\tau=\tau^{-1}dd^c\tau
                 -\tau^{-2}d\tau\wedge d^c\tau$$ and the vanishing of the pullback of $d\tau$ to $\partial E_s$ imply <a id="eq:mass-level-flux"></a>$$\label{eq:mass-level-flux}
s^{-d}\int_{E_s}(dd^c\tau)^d
 =\int_{\partial E_s}\gamma\wedge(d\gamma)^{d-1}.$$

<a id="eq:mass-punctured-flux"></a>
For sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$, the closed Euclidean ball $\overline{B_\varepsilon}$ is contained in $E_s$. Orient $S_\varepsilon=\partial B_\varepsilon$ outward from this ball. Applying Stokes on $E_s\setminus\overline{B_\varepsilon}$ gives $$\label{eq:mass-punctured-flux}
s^{-d}\int_{E_s}(dd^c\tau)^d
 -\int_{S_\varepsilon}\gamma\wedge(d\gamma)^{d-1}
 =\int_{E_s\setminus\overline{B_\varepsilon}}
                     (dd^c\log\tau)^d\geq0.$$ Thus it remains to determine the flux through a shrinking sphere.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
###### The homogeneous flux.

<a id="eq:mass-homogeneous-limit"></a>
Let $T(z)=|H(z)|^2$ and $S=\{z:|z|=1\}$. The holomorphic Taylor expansion implies $$\varepsilon^{-2k}\tau(\varepsilon z)\longrightarrow T(z)
\quad\text{in }C^2\text{ on a fixed closed annulus containing }S.$$ For example, termwise differentiation of the convergent Taylor series gives this convergence for the rescaled map and then for its squared norm. Since $T$ is strictly positive on that annulus, the logarithms converge in $C^2$ as well. Pulling back to $S$ by dilation, the additive constant $2k\log\varepsilon$ disappears under $d^c$. Consequently $$\label{eq:mass-homogeneous-limit}
\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}
 \int_{S_\varepsilon}\gamma\wedge(d\gamma)^{d-1}
 =\int_S\alpha\wedge(d\alpha)^{d-1},
\qquad \alpha=d^c\log T\big|_S.$$

We compare this flux with that of the radial function $|z|^{2k}$. Homogeneity gives $$T(rz)=r^{2k}T(z)\quad(r>0),\qquad T(e^{i\theta}z)=T(z).$$ Thus $\log T-k\log|z|^2$ is unchanged by dilation and by common phase rotation. These invariances will make its contribution to the flux vanish. On $S$, put $$\alpha_0=d^c\log|z|^2\big|_S,
\qquad
\beta=d^c\bigl(\log T-k\log|z|^2\bigr)\big|_S,
\qquad
\alpha_t=k\alpha_0+t\beta\quad(0\leq t\leq1).$$ Thus $\alpha_1=\alpha$. For the nowhere-zero tangent vector field $V(z)=iz$ on $S$, the dilation identity and <a href="#eq:mass-dc-convention" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-dc-convention">[eq:mass-dc-convention]</a> give $\beta(V)=0$ and $\alpha_0(V)=1/2$. The phase-rotation identity makes every $\alpha_t$ invariant under the flow of $V$. Cartan’s identity therefore gives $$\iota_Vd\alpha_t
 =\mathcal L_V\alpha_t-d\bigl(\alpha_t(V)\bigr)=0,$$ where $\iota_V$ denotes contraction and $\mathcal L_V$ the Lie derivative. It follows that the top-degree form $\beta\wedge(d\alpha_t)^{d-1}$ on the $(2d-1)$-dimensional sphere vanishes: its contraction with the nonzero vector $V$ is zero. Differentiation and integration by parts on the closed sphere now yield $$\frac{d}{dt}\int_S\alpha_t\wedge(d\alpha_t)^{d-1}
 =d\int_S\beta\wedge(d\alpha_t)^{d-1}=0.$$ For $d=1$ this is simply the derivative of $\int_S\alpha_t$; the same argument applies.

On the unit sphere, $\alpha_0$ and $d\alpha_0$ are the restrictions of $d^c|z|^2$ and $dd^c|z|^2$, respectively. Hence a final use of Stokes and <a href="#eq:mass-volume-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-volume-normalization">[eq:mass-volume-normalization]</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
\int_S\alpha\wedge(d\alpha)^{d-1}
 &=k^d\int_S\alpha_0\wedge(d\alpha_0)^{d-1}\\
 &=k^d\int_{\{|z|<1\}}(dd^c|z|^2)^d\\
 &=k^d d!\,\operatorname{vol}_{2d}(\{|z|<1\})
  =(\pi k)^d.
\end{aligned}$$

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
###### The minor bound.

Equations <a href="#eq:mass-punctured-flux" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-punctured-flux">[eq:mass-punctured-flux]</a> and <a href="#eq:mass-homogeneous-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-homogeneous-limit">[eq:mass-homogeneous-limit]</a> imply $$\int_{E_s}(dd^c\tau)^d\geq s^d(\pi k)^d$$ for every regular $s\in(0,1)$. Sard’s theorem supplies an increasing sequence of such values tending to $1$. Since the integrand is nonnegative, monotone convergence gives $$\int_{\{\tau<1\}}(dd^c\tau)^d\geq(\pi k)^d.$$ Finally, the complex Hessian $Z^*Z$ and Cauchy–Binet give the identity $$(dd^c\tau)^d
 =d!\sum_{|I|=d}|\det Z_I|^2\,dV.$$ Division by $d!$ proves <a href="#eq:holomorphic-minor-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:holomorphic-minor-mass">[eq:holomorphic-minor-mass]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:feasibility"></a>
### Feasible bases

We now apply the holomorphic mass estimate to finitely many real strip constraints. It gives a lower bound for the sum of their boundary feasibility probabilities. For the lens of Lemma <a href="#lem:planar-lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:planar-lens">6</a>, these probabilities will become volumes of real simplices.

<a id="eq:strip-body"></a>
Fix $d\geq1$ and nonzero real row vectors $b_1,\ldots,b_m$ spanning $(\mathbb R^d)^*$. Write $$\label{eq:strip-body}
 A=\{X\in\mathbb R^d:|b_iX|\leq1\text{ for every }i\},
 \qquad C=A^\circ=\operatorname{conv}\{\pm b_1,\ldots,\pm b_m\}.$$ We identify rows with vectors when taking a convex hull, and extend their action complex-linearly to $\mathbb C^d$. The spanning assumption makes $A$ bounded, and $0\in\operatorname{int}A$. The polar identity follows from the bipolar theorem: the polar of the displayed convex hull is exactly $A$. Let $\mathcal B$ be the collection of $d$-element sets of independent rows. For $I\in\mathcal B$, put $B_I=(b_i)_{i\in I}$ in increasing index order and $D_I=|\det B_I|$. Dependent row sets are omitted throughout.

<a id="eq:feasible-probabilities"></a>
For the next lemma, $F$ may be any biholomorphism of the unit disk onto a bounded planar domain $D$, with $F(0)=0$ and a continuous extension to the closed disk. For independent uniform angles $\theta_i\in[0,2\pi)$, $i\in I$, define $$\label{eq:feasible-probabilities}
 Z_I(\theta)=B_I^{-1}\bigl(F(e^{i\theta_i})\bigr)_{i\in I},
 \qquad
 P_I=\mathbb P\{b_jZ_I(\theta)\in\overline D\text{ for all }j\},
