 \qquad S=\sum_{I\in\mathcal B}P_I.$$ The active coordinates $i\in I$ already belong to $\overline D$; thus the probability tests only the remaining rows.

<a id="stmt-lem-holomorphic-feasibility"></a>
<div id="lem:holomorphic-feasibility" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Boundary feasibility). *Let $d\ge1$ and let $b_1,\ldots,b_m$ be nonzero real rows spanning $(\mathbb R^d)^*$. Let $F:\mathbb D\to D$ be a biholomorphism onto a bounded planar domain, continuous on $\overline{\mathbb D}$, with $F(0)=0$. Then the sum $S$ in <a href="#eq:feasible-probabilities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:feasible-probabilities">[eq:feasible-probabilities]</a> satisfies $S\ge1$. No condition on extra-row boundary equalities or on dependent row subsets is required.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-holomorphic-feasibility"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-holomorphic-feasibility</summary>

<a id="eq:feasibility-mass"></a>
*Proof.* Let $g=F^{-1}$ and set $$\Omega=\{z\in\mathbb C^d:b_jz\in D\text{ for every }j\},
 \qquad h_j(z)=g(b_jz).$$ The set $\Omega$ is open, bounded, and contains $0$. For an integer $k\geq1$, apply Lemma <a href="#lem:holomorphic-mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:holomorphic-mass">8</a> to the holomorphic map $$f_k=(h_1^k,\ldots,h_m^k),\qquad
 \tau_k=\sum_{j=1}^m|h_j|^{2k}.$$ We shall let $k$ tend to infinity. The mass lower bound has scale $k^d$. The radial substitution below removes the same factor from the Jacobian integrals and leaves a fixed domain on which the inferred points tend to boundary samples. The unique zero of $f_k$ is $0$: $g$ vanishes only at $0$, and the rows span. Writing $c=g'(0)\ne0$, its degree-$k$ leading term is $((cb_1z)^k,\ldots,(cb_mz)^k)$, also nonzero whenever $z\ne0$. For $0<s<1$, the set $\{\tau_k\leq s\}$ is compact in $\Omega$. Indeed each of its row coordinates belongs to the fixed compact set $F(\{|w|\leq s^{1/(2k)}\})\subset D$. A basis of rows bounds $z$; limits retain all these inclusions and the inequality $\tau_k\leq s$. All hypotheses of the mass lemma therefore hold. The derivative row of $h_j^k$ is a scalar multiple of $b_j$, so dependent row subsets have zero minors. Consequently $$\label{eq:feasibility-mass}
 \sum_{I\in\mathcal B}M_{I,k}\geq
 k^d\frac{\pi^d}{d!},\qquad
 M_{I,k}:=\int_{\{\tau_k<1\}}
 \left|\det\frac{\partial(h_i^k)_{i\in I}}
                        {\partial(z_1,\ldots,z_d)}\right|^2\,dV_{2d}(z).$$

Fix $I$. The change of variables $u_i=h_i(z)$, $i\in I$, is one-to-one onto its image, with inverse $z=B_I^{-1}(F(u_i))_{i\in I}$. Its squared complex Jacobian is its real volume Jacobian. The squared determinant in $M_{I,k}$ is this Jacobian factor times $k^{2d}\prod_i|u_i|^{2k-2}$. The transformed integration domain is contained in $$\left\{u\in\mathbb D^d:
       \sum_{i\in I}|u_i|^{2k}<1,\quad
       b_jB_I^{-1}(F(u_i))_{i\in I}\in D\text{ for all }j\right\}.$$ We use this larger domain for an upper bound, and then put $u_i=\rho_i^{1/k}e^{i\theta_i}$. The coordinate hyperplanes, on which polar coordinates are undefined, have Lebesgue measure zero. Since $$k^2|u_i|^{2k-2}\,dV_2(u_i)
      =k\rho_i\,d\rho_i\,d\theta_i,$$ we obtain <a id="eq:feasibility-radial"></a>$$\label{eq:feasibility-radial}
 \frac{M_{I,k}}{k^d}\leq
 \int_{\substack{\rho_i>0,\ \sum_i\rho_i^2<1\\
                  \theta\in[0,2\pi)^d}}
 \mathbf1\!\left\{
  b_jB_I^{-1}\bigl(F(\rho_i^{1/k}e^{i\theta_i})\bigr)_{i\in I}
                    \in D\text{ for every }j\right\}
 \prod_{i\in I}\rho_i\,d\rho_i\,d\theta_i.$$

The measure in this integral is finite: its total mass is the volume $\pi^d/d!$ of the unit ball in $\mathbb C^d$. For every positive $\rho_i$, continuity of $F$ on the closed disk makes the inferred points converge to $Z_I(\theta)$. If a limiting row coordinate lies outside $\overline D$, that constraint fails for all sufficiently large $k$. Thus the upper limit of the indicator is bounded by the indicator of the event defining $P_I$, including all possible boundary equalities. The upper-bound form of Fatou’s lemma, applied to functions bounded by $1$ on this finite measure space, gives $$\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{M_{I,k}}{k^d}
 \leq \frac{\pi^d}{d!}P_I.$$ There are only finitely many bases, so <a href="#eq:feasibility-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:feasibility-mass">[eq:feasibility-mass]</a> and these upper bounds give $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{\pi^d}{d!}
 &\leq\limsup_{k\to\infty}
          \sum_{I\in\mathcal B}\frac{M_{I,k}}{k^d}\\
 &\leq\sum_{I\in\mathcal B}
          \limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{M_{I,k}}{k^d}\\
 &\leq\frac{\pi^d}{d!}\sum_{I\in\mathcal B}P_I.
\end{aligned}$$ Thus $S\geq1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Boundary equalities for the lens

The lower bound for $S$ allows additional boundary equalities. The real volume identity in Section <a href="#sec:simplex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:simplex">6</a> will need the stronger fact that these equalities have probability zero. From now on, $F,D$, and $\lambda$ are those of Lemma <a href="#lem:planar-lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:planar-lens">6</a>. We may also require that no two rows are proportional. For any finite strip presentation this is achieved without changing $A$ by keeping, on each row line, a row of largest magnitude; its strip implies all the discarded strips on that line. Zero rows, if present initially, impose no constraint and are discarded. In dimension one the reduced list has a single row, so there are no extra-row boundary equalities to consider.

<a id="stmt-lem-boundary-ties"></a>
<div id="lem:boundary-ties" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (Additional boundary equalities have probability zero). *Let $F:\mathbb D\to D$ be the lens map of Lemma <a href="#lem:planar-lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:planar-lens">6</a>. Assume that the nonzero spanning rows $b_1,\ldots,b_m$ are pairwise nonproportional. For every $I\in\mathcal B$ and $j\notin I$, $$\mathbb P\{b_jZ_I(\theta)\in\partial D\}=0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-boundary-ties"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-boundary-ties</summary>

*Proof.* Write $b_jB_I^{-1}=(c_i)_{i\in I}$. At least two coefficients, say $c_a,c_b$, are nonzero; otherwise $b_j$ would be proportional to a basis row. Let $\gamma(\theta)=F(e^{i\theta})$. Apart from its two endpoints, the lens boundary consists of the smooth regular branches $(\pm\lambda(t),t)$, $-1<t<1$. Their tangent directions have at most one occurrence on each branch for any fixed unoriented line direction, because $\lambda'$ is strictly monotone. The boundary parameter has constant nonzero speed in $t$ on each branch, as established in the proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:planar-lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:planar-lens">6</a>.

Hold all phases other than $\theta_a,\theta_b$ fixed. The map $$(\theta_a,\theta_b)\longmapsto
   c_a\gamma(\theta_a)+c_b\gamma(\theta_b)
       +\sum_{i\ne a,b}c_i\gamma(\theta_i)$$ has real Jacobian determinant $\det(c_a\gamma'(\theta_a),c_b\gamma'(\theta_b))$. For every nonendpoint $\theta_a$, it vanishes at only finitely many nonendpoint values of $\theta_b$. Its zero set, including the excluded endpoint lines, therefore has planar measure zero by Fubini’s theorem. Off that set the inverse function theorem gives local diffeomorphism charts. A countable subcover of such charts suffices, and in each chart the inverse is locally Lipschitz, so it sends a planar null set to a null set. The set $\partial D$ is planar-null, being a countable union of compact smooth subarcs and two points. Its inverse image consequently has measure zero in the two remaining phases. A final application of Fubini over the fixed phases proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:simplex"></a>
### The real simplex identity

The lens boundary law now turns the feasibility sum into an exact integral over the strip body. The simplices in this integral lie in the polar body; their missing volume will also be the quantity used in the equality argument.

<a id="eq:feasible-simplex-union"></a>
Keep the nonzero spanning, pairwise nonproportional rows of <a href="#eq:strip-body" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:strip-body">[eq:strip-body]</a>. For $X\in\operatorname{int}A$, define <a id="eq:real-feasibility-body"></a>$$\label{eq:real-feasibility-body}
 L_X=\{Y\in\mathbb R^d:|b_jY|\leq\lambda(b_jX)
                         \text{ for every }j\}.$$ All widths are strictly positive, so $L_X$ contains a neighborhood of $0$; a basis of rows shows that it is bounded. It is therefore a convex polytope with nonempty interior. For a basis $I$ and signs $\epsilon=(\epsilon_i)_{i\in I}\in\{-1,1\}^I$, let $$Y_{I,\epsilon}(X)=B_I^{-1}
          \bigl(\epsilon_i\lambda(b_iX)\bigr)_{i\in I}.$$ We call $(I,\epsilon)$ feasible at $X$ when this vector belongs to $L_X$. This definition specifies a unique vector for every choice, whether or not it is feasible. Set $$\label{eq:feasible-simplex-union}
 T_{I,\epsilon}=\operatorname{conv}
                  \bigl(0,\{\epsilon_i b_i:i\in I\}\bigr),
 \qquad
 \Sigma_X=\bigcup_{(I,\epsilon)\text{ feasible at }X}T_{I,\epsilon}.$$ Thus $\Sigma_X$ is a finite union of closed $d$-simplices contained in $C=A^\circ$. This definition applies at every interior $X$, including points with additional tight constraints. For integration only, declare every pair infeasible and set $\Sigma_X=\varnothing$ when $X\in\partial A$.

A feasible pair specifies a vertex $Y_{I,\epsilon}(X)$ of $L_X$. A strictly positive combination of its signed tight rows defines a linear functional maximized uniquely at that vertex. This is why different feasible pairs give simplices with disjoint interiors whenever their vertices are distinct. The proof below removes, outside a null set of $X$, the boundary ties that could make two pairs specify the same vertex.

<a id="stmt-prop-simplex-integral"></a>
<div id="prop:simplex-integral" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:simplex-integral"></a>
<a id="eq:simplex-deficit"></a>
**Proposition 11** (Feasible-simplex identity). *Let $b_1,\ldots,b_m$ be nonzero, pairwise nonproportional real rows spanning $(\mathbb R^d)^*$, and let $F,D,\lambda$ be as in Lemma <a href="#lem:planar-lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:planar-lens">6</a>. With $A$, $S$, and $\Sigma_X$ defined in <a href="#eq:strip-body" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:strip-body">[eq:strip-body]</a>, <a href="#eq:feasible-probabilities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:feasible-probabilities">[eq:feasible-probabilities]</a>, and <a href="#eq:feasible-simplex-union" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:feasible-simplex-union">[eq:feasible-simplex-union]</a>, respectively, $$\label{eq:simplex-integral}
 1\leq S
   =\frac{d!}{4^d}\int_A|\Sigma_X|\,dX
   \leq\frac{d!}{4^d}|A|\,|A^\circ|.$$ In particular, $|A|\,|A^\circ|\geq4^d/d!$, and $$\label{eq:simplex-deficit}
 0\leq\int_A\bigl(|A^\circ|-|\Sigma_X|\bigr)\,dX
       \leq |A|\,|A^\circ|-\frac{4^d}{d!}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-simplex-integral"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-simplex-integral</summary>

<a id="eq:probability-real-integral"></a>
*Proof.* We first convert each probability into a real integral. By <a href="#eq:lens-boundary-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lens-boundary-law">[eq:lens-boundary-law]</a>, each active boundary coordinate has the law $\epsilon_i\lambda(t_i)+it_i$, with $t_i$ uniform on $[-1,1]$ and $\epsilon_i$ an independent fair sign. Consequently each fixed sign choice has joint measure $4^{-d}\,dt_I$ on $[-1,1]^d$. Writing the inferred complex vector as $Y+iX$ gives $$t_I=B_IX,\qquad Y=Y_{I,\epsilon}(X).$$ The constraints that all row coordinates lie in $\overline D$ are exactly $X\in A$ and $Y\in L_X$, apart from the immaterial boundary $\partial A$. This boundary has measure zero, since it is contained in the finitely many hyperplanes $b_jX=\pm1$. The change of variables $t_I=B_IX$ therefore gives $$\label{eq:probability-real-integral}
 P_I=\frac{D_I}{4^d}\int_A
       \sum_{\epsilon\in\{-1,1\}^I}
         \mathbf1\{(I,\epsilon)\text{ is feasible at }X\}\,dX.$$

For almost every $X\in\operatorname{int}A$, every feasible pair $(I,\epsilon)$ has no tight constraint outside $I$. Indeed, such an additional equality means that an extra row of $Y+iX$ lies on $\partial D$. Lemma <a href="#lem:boundary-ties" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:boundary-ties">10</a> makes this event null in the basis phases; the boundary law and the invertible change $t_I=B_IX$ make its set of $X$ null for each fixed sign choice. There are finitely many bases, sign choices, and extra rows, so a single null set removes all these equalities.

Fix $X$ outside this null set. Distinct feasible pairs have distinct vectors $Y_{I,\epsilon}(X)$. If their bases differ, a shared vector would have a tight row outside one of the bases. If their bases agree but some signs differ, the shared vector would satisfy opposite equations with the same strictly positive width, which is impossible.

<a id="eq:simplex-volume-sum"></a>
The interiors of their simplices are disjoint. To see this, let $e\in\operatorname{int}T_{I,\epsilon}$. It has a representation $e=\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_i\epsilon_i b_i$ with all $\alpha_i>0$ and $\sum_i\alpha_i<1$. For every $Y\in L_X$, $$eY\leq\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_i\lambda(b_iX),$$ and equality holds at $Y_{I,\epsilon}(X)$. Equality forces every independent equation $\epsilon_i b_iY=\lambda(b_iX)$, so this is the unique maximizer of the linear functional $e$ on $L_X$. If $e$ belonged to the interiors of two feasible simplices, their distinct vectors would both be its unique maximizer, a contradiction. Each simplex has volume $D_I/d!$, and their boundaries have measure zero. It follows that, for almost every $X$, $$\label{eq:simplex-volume-sum}
 d!\,|\Sigma_X|
      =\sum_{I\in\mathcal B}\sum_{\epsilon\in\{-1,1\}^I}
               D_I\,\mathbf1\{(I,\epsilon)\text{ is feasible at }X\}.$$

All integrals here are measurable. For each pair, feasibility is a finite collection of closed inequalities between continuous functions of $X\in\operatorname{int}A$. In particular the set $$\{(X,e):X\in\operatorname{int}A,\ e\in\Sigma_X\}$$ is a finite union of products of Borel feasibility sets and fixed closed simplices. Fubini’s theorem makes $X\mapsto|\Sigma_X|$ measurable, and it is bounded by $|C|$. Sum <a href="#eq:probability-real-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:probability-real-integral">[eq:probability-real-integral]</a>, use <a href="#eq:simplex-volume-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:simplex-volume-sum">[eq:simplex-volume-sum]</a>, and apply Lemma <a href="#lem:holomorphic-feasibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:holomorphic-feasibility">9</a>. This proves the first equality and lower bound in <a href="#eq:simplex-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:simplex-integral">[eq:simplex-integral]</a>. The containment $\Sigma_X\subset C$ proves the upper bound. In particular, the missing volume has the exact expression $$\int_A\bigl(|C|-|\Sigma_X|\bigr)\,dX
   =|A|\,|C|-\frac{4^d}{d!}S.$$ It is nonnegative by containment, and $S\geq1$ bounds it above by $|A|\,|C|-4^d/d!$. This proves <a href="#eq:simplex-deficit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:simplex-deficit">[eq:simplex-deficit]</a> and identifies the quantity that will tend to zero in the equality argument. ◻

</details>

For a concrete union with positive missing volume, take $b_1=(1,0)$, $b_2=(1/2,\sqrt3/2)$, $b_3=b_2-b_1$, and $X=0.9(1,1/\sqrt3)$, as in Figure <a href="#fig:feasible-simplex-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:feasible-simplex-deficit">2</a>. Then $b_1X=b_2X=0.9$ and $b_3X=0$. Write $a=\lambda(0.9)$. The endpoint integral for $\lambda$ and $\cot x<1/x$ for $0<x<\pi/2$ give $$a<\frac{\log(40/\pi)+1}{5\pi}<\frac4{15},
 \qquad
 \lambda(0)>\frac8{\pi^2}\left(1-\frac19\right)
             >\frac{32}{45}.$$ The second bound uses the alternating series for $F(1)$. In particular, $2a<\lambda(0)$, so the third strip in the example is strictly redundant.

<figure id="fig:feasible-simplex-deficit">
<embed src="figures/feasible-simplex-deficit.pdf" />
<figcaption>A feasible-simplex union can leave part of the polar uncovered. Here $b_{1} = (1,0)$, $b_2=(1/2,\sqrt3/2)$, $b_3=(-1/2,\sqrt3/2)$, $C = conv { ± b_{1},  ± b_{2},  ± b_{3}}$, and $X=0.9(1,1/\sqrt3)$. The third strip is redundant in $L_{X}$, as verified above. The four witnesses $Y_{ϵ1}ϵ_{2}$, defined by $b_{i}Y = ϵ_{i}a$ for $i = 1, 2$, correspond to the four triangles $conv (0,ϵ_{1}b_{1},ϵ_{2}b_{2})$ with matching sign labels. Their union is $Σ_{X} = conv { ± b_{1},  ± b_{2}}$. The two hatched caps have total area $\sqrt3/2$, one third of $|C|$.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-feasible-simplex-deficit"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:feasible-simplex-deficit`)

The source figure is an external graphic; no image payload is embedded.

Visual witness description of PDF page 17 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Two diagrams side by side: (a) labelled "(a) Feasible witnesses in $L_X$" showing a region $L_X$ between bounding lines $b_2 Y = a$ (upper left) and $b_1 Y = a$ (lower right) with four vertices $Y_{-+}$, $Y_{++}$, $Y_{--}$, and $Y_{+-}$ connected by dashed lines forming a parallelogram; below are the formulas $\max_{Y \in L_X} |b_3 Y| = 2a < \lambda(0)$ and $a = \lambda(0.9)$; (b) labelled "(b) Feasible simplices in $C$" showing a regular hexagon $C$ with vertices $b_1, b_2, b_3, -b_1, -b_2, -b_3$ centered at $0$, containing four shaded triangles meeting at $0$ with sign labels $++$, $-+$, $+-$, $--$ forming parallelogram $\Sigma_X = \operatorname{conv}\{\pm b_1, \pm b_2\}$, and two horizontally hatched caps at top and bottom; below are the expressions $\Sigma_X = \operatorname{conv}\{\pm b_1, \pm b_2\}$ and $|C \setminus \Sigma_X| = \sqrt{3}/2$.
```


<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Approximation of an arbitrary convex body

<a id="stmt-cor-symmetric-inequality"></a>
<div id="cor:symmetric-inequality" class="corollary">

**Corollary 12** (The symmetric volume-product inequality). *For every origin-symmetric convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^d$, $d\geq1$, $$|K|\,|K^\circ|\geq\frac{4^d}{d!}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-symmetric-inequality"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-symmetric-inequality</summary>

<a id="eq:polar-approximation"></a>
*Proof.* Put $Q=K^\circ$ and choose $a>0$ with $aB_2^d\subset Q$, where $B_2^d$ is the Euclidean unit ball. For $\varepsilon_j\downarrow0$, take a finite $\varepsilon_j$-net in $Q$, adjoin its negatives, and let $Q_j$ be its convex hull. Thus $Q_j\subset Q$, and their support functions satisfy, for every Euclidean unit vector $u$, $$h_Q(u)-\varepsilon_j\leq h_{Q_j}(u)\leq h_Q(u),
 \qquad h_Q(u)\geq a.$$ For $\delta_j=\varepsilon_j/a<1$, this implies $$\label{eq:polar-approximation}
 (1-\delta_j)Q\subset Q_j\subset Q,
 \qquad
 K\subset A_j:=Q_j^\circ\subset(1-\delta_j)^{-1}K.$$ The first inclusions follow from the support-function inequalities and convex separation; the second follow by polarity. In particular $Q_j$ and $A_j$ are full-dimensional symmetric polytopes for large $j$.

Choose one vertex from each antipodal vertex pair of $Q_j$ as a row list. These rows are nonzero and span. They are pairwise nonproportional: two distinct boundary vertices on the same ray from an interior origin cannot both be extreme, and the antipodal duplication has already been removed. Their strip body is $A_j$. Proposition <a href="#prop:simplex-integral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:simplex-integral">11</a> therefore gives $|A_j|\,|Q_j|\geq4^d/d!$. The homothetic bounds <a href="#eq:polar-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:polar-approximation">[eq:polar-approximation]</a> show both $|Q_j|\to|Q|$ and $|A_j|\to|K|$, because dilation by $t>0$ multiplies volume by $t^d$. Passing to the limit proves the assertion. No smoothness, simplicity, or general-position assumption is imposed on $K$. In dimension one the assertion can also be read directly: $K=[-a,a]$ has polar $[-1/a,1/a]$ and product $4$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:zero-deficit"></a>
### Equality and a common feasibility witness

The integral in <a href="#eq:simplex-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:simplex-integral">[eq:simplex-integral]</a> measures how much of the polar body is covered by feasible simplices. For a body attaining the sharp volume product, we shall make the uncovered volume tend to zero after adjoining one segment. Compactness then gives a feasible witness at every interior point and in every direction. The resulting representation will be the input to the equality classification.

<a id="eq:segment-lift"></a>
Throughout this section $K=-K\subset\mathbb R^n$ is a convex body, $n\ge1$, satisfying $$|K|\,|K^\circ|=\frac{4^n}{n!}.$$ Set $Q=K^\circ$, $d=n+1$, and $$\label{eq:segment-lift}
 B=K\oplus_1[-1,1]
   =\{(x,s):p_K(x)+|s|\le1\},
 \qquad C=B^\circ=Q\times[-1,1].$$ The polar $C$ has the two horizontal faces $Q\times\{1\}$ and $Q\times\{-1\}$. The representations constructed below will use vectors from these faces. In this lifted space, $X$ and $Y$ belong to $\mathbb R^d$, while elements of $C$ act on them by the Euclidean pairing. Recall that the lens width $\lambda:[-1,1]\to[0,\infty)$ is continuous, even and concave, with $\lambda(0)>0$ and $\lambda(t)\le\lambda(0)$.

<a id="stmt-prop-optimal-representation"></a>
<div id="prop:optimal-representation" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:optimal-representation"></a>
<a id="eq:common-witness"></a>
<a id="eq:optimal-cost"></a>
**Proposition 13** (A representation minimizing the lens cost). *Let $K\subset\mathbb R^n$, $n\ge1$, be an origin-symmetric convex body with $P(K)=4^n/n!$. Put $Q=K^\circ$, $d=n+1$, and define the lifted bodies $B,C$ by <a href="#eq:segment-lift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:segment-lift">[eq:segment-lift]</a>. For every $X\in\operatorname{int}B$ and every nonzero $\xi=(q,a)\in\mathbb R^n\times\mathbb R$, put $$T=p_C(\xi)=\max\{p_Q(q),|a|\}.$$ There are $1\le r\le d$ vectors $c_i=(b_i,\sigma_i)$ with $b_i\in Q$ and $\sigma_i\in\{-1,1\}$, nonnegative numbers $\alpha_i$, and a vector $Y\in\mathbb R^d$, such that $$\label{eq:optimal-representation}
 \xi=\sum_{i=1}^r\alpha_i c_i,
 \qquad \sum_{i=1}^r\alpha_i=T,$$ and $$\label{eq:common-witness}
 |h\cdot Y|\le\lambda(h\cdot X)\quad(h\in C),
 \qquad
 c_i\cdot Y=\lambda(c_i\cdot X)\quad(1\le i\le r).$$ In particular, for every finite representation $\xi=\sum_{j=1}^s\beta_jh_j$ with $\beta_j\ge0$ and $h_j\in C$, $$\label{eq:optimal-cost}
 \sum_{i=1}^r\alpha_i\lambda(c_i\cdot X)
 \le \sum_{j=1}^s\beta_j\lambda(h_j\cdot X).$$ The choices may depend on $X$ and $\xi$; the same choice satisfies <a href="#eq:optimal-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:optimal-cost">[eq:optimal-cost]</a> for all the competing representations.*

</div>

The last coordinates $\sigma_i$ split the chosen rows into two groups. In Section <a href="#sec:median" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:median">8</a>, we compare representation costs as $X$ approaches the apex $(0,1)$ of $B$; the endpoint behavior of $\lambda$ makes the two signs contribute differently. We now prove Proposition <a href="#prop:optimal-representation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:optimal-representation">13</a> in three steps: approximate $C$ by polytopes, locate feasible simplices near each prescribed point and direction, and pass to a common witness to compare costs.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Inner polytopes and vanishing missing volume

<a id="eq:lift-equality"></a>
The horizontal section of $B$ at height $s\in[-1,1]$ is $(1-|s|)K$. Hence $$|B|=|K|\int_{-1}^1(1-|s|)^n\,ds
     =\frac{2|K|}{n+1},
 \qquad |C|=2|Q|,$$ and therefore $$\label{eq:lift-equality}
 |B|\,|C|=\frac4{n+1}|K|\,|Q|=\frac{4^d}{d!}.$$

Choose full-dimensional symmetric polytopes $Q_j\subset Q$ converging to $Q$ in Hausdorff distance. For example, take the convex hulls of increasingly fine finite symmetric nets in $Q$, including a fixed spanning symmetric set. There are numbers $t_j\in(0,1)$ with $t_j\to1$ such that $$t_jQ\subseteq Q_j\subseteq Q.$$ Indeed, if $Q$ contains a Euclidean ball of radius $r_0>0$ and the Hausdorff error is $\varepsilon_j$, then on Euclidean unit directions $$h_{Q_j}\ge h_Q-\varepsilon_j
          \ge (1-\varepsilon_j/r_0)h_Q.$$ The support-function criterion for containment gives the claim, after discarding finitely many indices and choosing $t_j$ slightly smaller if necessary.

<a id="eq:lift-sandwich"></a>
Put $C_j=Q_j\times[-1,1]$ and $B_j=C_j^\circ$. The inclusions above give $$\label{eq:lift-sandwich}
 t_jC\subseteq C_j\subseteq C,
 \qquad
 B\subseteq B_j\subseteq t_j^{-1}B.$$ In particular, $|B_j|\,|C_j|\to|B|\,|C|$. Use one row from each antipodal vertex pair of $C_j$ to describe $B_j$ as an intersection of strips. These rows span $\mathbb R^d$, and no two are proportional: a ray from the interior point $0$ meets the boundary of a convex body in only one point. Every signed row is a vertex of $C_j$, and thus belongs to $Q_j\times\{-1,1\}$.

<a id="eq:vanishing-missing-volume"></a>
Let $\Sigma_{j,X}$ be the union of the feasible signed basis simplices for this strip description, as in <a href="#eq:simplex-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:simplex-integral">[eq:simplex-integral]</a>. In particular, for every $X\in\operatorname{int}B_j$ it is a finite union of closed simplices contained in $C_j$. Its definition is retained also at the exceptional interior points where several constraints are simultaneously tight. The integral estimate and <a href="#eq:lift-equality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift-equality">[eq:lift-equality]</a> imply $$\label{eq:vanishing-missing-volume}
 \begin{split}
 0\le D_j
 &:=\int_{B_j}\bigl(|C_j|-|\Sigma_{j,X}|\bigr)\,dX\\
 &\le |B_j|\,|C_j|-\frac{4^d}{d!}
 \longrightarrow0.
 \end{split}$$

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### From an integral deficit to each point and direction

<a id="eq:nearby-feasible-pairs"></a>
Fix $X\in\operatorname{int}B$ and $e\in\partial C$. We first construct indices $j_k\to\infty$ and points $$\label{eq:nearby-feasible-pairs}
 X_k\in\operatorname{int}B_{j_k},\qquad
 e_k\in\Sigma_{j_k,X_k},\qquad
 (X_k,e_k)\longrightarrow(X,e).$$ This is a consequence of <a href="#eq:vanishing-missing-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:vanishing-missing-volume">[eq:vanishing-missing-volume]</a> on product neighborhoods; it does not require a pointwise limit of the functions $X\mapsto|\Sigma_{j,X}|$.

For each $k$, choose a Euclidean ball $U_k$ of positive volume contained in $\operatorname{int}B\cap B(X,1/k)$. There is also a ball $V_k$ of positive volume with closure contained in $\operatorname{int}C\cap B(e,1/k)$. To see this at the boundary point $e$, first move a small distance from $e$ toward $0$; the resulting point is interior because $0\in\operatorname{int}C$. Take a sufficiently small ball about that point. By <a href="#eq:lift-sandwich" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift-sandwich">[eq:lift-sandwich]</a>, $U_k\subset\operatorname{int}B_j$ for every $j$, and $V_k\subset C_j$ for all sufficiently large $j$. The second assertion follows also from $\max_{h\in\overline{V_k}}p_C(h)<1$ and $t_j\to1$.

Choose $j_k>j_{k-1}$ so large that these inclusions hold and $D_{j_k}<|U_k|\,|V_k|$. If $\Sigma_{j_k,Z}$ missed $V_k$ for every $Z\in U_k$, its missing volume would be at least $|V_k|$ throughout $U_k$, contradicting this inequality. Thus choose $X_k\in U_k$ and $e_k\in\Sigma_{j_k,X_k}\cap V_k$. This proves <a href="#eq:nearby-feasible-pairs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:nearby-feasible-pairs">[eq:nearby-feasible-pairs]</a>.

Choose one feasible simplex containing $e_k$. Its $d$ signed basis rows, written $c_{1k},\ldots,c_{dk}\in Q_{j_k}\times\{-1,1\}$, give weights $\theta_{ik}\ge0$ satisfying <a id="eq:approximate-representation"></a>$$\label{eq:approximate-representation}
 e_k=\sum_{i=1}^d\theta_{ik}c_{ik},
 \qquad \sum_{i=1}^d\theta_{ik}\le1.$$ The coefficient left over is the weight of the simplex vertex $0$. Feasibility supplies one vector $Y_k$ such that $$c_{ik}\cdot Y_k=\lambda(c_{ik}\cdot X_k)\quad(1\le i\le d),
 \qquad
 |v\cdot Y_k|\le\lambda(v\cdot X_k)
 \quad\text{for every vertex $v$ of $C_{j_k}$}.$$ Here a sign on a basis row has been absorbed using the evenness of $\lambda$.

The last inequality extends to all $h\in C_{j_k}$. Indeed, write $h=\sum_\ell s_\ell v_\ell$ as a convex combination of vertices. The triangle inequality followed by concavity gives $$|h\cdot Y_k|
 \le\sum_\ell s_\ell|v_\ell\cdot Y_k|
 \le\sum_\ell s_\ell\lambda(v_\ell\cdot X_k)
 \le\lambda(h\cdot X_k).$$ Consequently $Y_k\in\lambda(0)B_{j_k}$. The polar sandwich <a href="#eq:lift-sandwich" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift-sandwich">[eq:lift-sandwich]</a> makes these witnesses uniformly bounded, even if the selected bases approach singularity.

<a id="eq:limiting-feasibility"></a>
Pass to a subsequence on which $Y_k\to Y$, every $c_{ik}\to c_i$, and every $\theta_{ik}\to\theta_i$. This uses a fixed finite product of compact sets: all rows belong to $Q\times\{-1,1\}$ and all weights belong to $[0,1]$. No independence of the limiting rows is needed. We have $$c_i\in Q\times\{-1,1\},\qquad
 e=\sum_{i=1}^d\theta_i c_i,\qquad
 \theta_i\ge0,\qquad \sum_i\theta_i\le1,$$ and continuity of $\lambda$ gives $c_i\cdot Y=\lambda(c_i\cdot X)$ for all $i$. For arbitrary $h\in C$, use $h_k=t_{j_k}h\in C_{j_k}$ in the feasibility inequality and pass to the limit. It follows that $$\label{eq:limiting-feasibility}
 |h\cdot Y|\le\lambda(h\cdot X)\qquad(h\in C).$$ The subsequence has already been fixed; this argument proves <a href="#eq:limiting-feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limiting-feasibility">[eq:limiting-feasibility]</a> for all $h$ with the same $Y$.

Since $e\in\partial C$, its gauge is one. Therefore $$1=p_C(e)\le\sum_{i=1}^d\theta_i p_C(c_i)
   \le\sum_{i=1}^d\theta_i\le1.$$ Thus $\sum_i\theta_i=1$: none of the limiting mass remains at the simplex vertex $0$.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Comparison with every positive representation

To prove Proposition <a href="#prop:optimal-representation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:optimal-representation">13</a>, fix its $X$ and nonzero $\xi$, and apply the preceding construction to $e=\xi/T\in\partial C$. Set $\alpha_i=T\theta_i$ and discard any zero weights if desired. This gives at most $d$ rows and proves <a href="#eq:optimal-representation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:optimal-representation">[eq:optimal-representation]</a> and <a href="#eq:common-witness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:common-witness">[eq:common-witness]</a>. For any finite competing representation $\xi=\sum_j\beta_jh_j$, $\beta_j\ge0$, $h_j\in C$, the common witness gives $$\sum_i\alpha_i\lambda(c_i\cdot X)
 =\xi\cdot Y
 =\sum_j\beta_j h_j\cdot Y
 \le\sum_j\beta_j\lambda(h_j\cdot X).$$ This proves <a href="#eq:optimal-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:optimal-cost">[eq:optimal-cost]</a> and completes the proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:optimal-representation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:optimal-representation">13</a>. In particular, the conclusion holds for each prescribed $X$ and $\xi$, including directions for which every limiting representation is degenerate.

<a id="sec:median"></a>
### From endpoint costs to metric medians

We now convert the optimal representations into a geometric property of the polar body. A *metric median* of three points in a normed space is a point that lies between every pair: if the points are $x_0,x_1,x_2$, a median $m$ satisfies $$\|x_i-x_j\|=\|x_i-m\|+\|m-x_j\|
 \qquad(0\le i<j\le2).$$ The median need not be unique. Its existence will imply the ball intersection property that characterizes linear Hanner bodies.

<a id="stmt-prop-metric-median"></a>
<div id="prop:metric-median" class="proposition">

**Proposition 14** (Medians of an equality body). *Let $K\subset\mathbb R^n$, $n\ge1$, be an origin-symmetric convex body with $P(K)=4^n/n!$, and put $Q=K^\circ$. Every triple of points in $(\mathbb R^n,p_Q)$ has a metric median.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-metric-median"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-metric-median</summary>

*Proof.* Write $\|\cdot\|=p_Q$. Translation reduces the triple to $0,x,y$. If two points coincide, that repeated point is a median, so assume that $0,x,y$ are distinct. Set $$p_1=\|x\|,\qquad p_2=\|y\|,\qquad p=\|x-y\|,
 \qquad s=p_1+p_2,\qquad a=p_1-p_2.$$ All three distances are positive. The triangle and reverse triangle inequalities give $s\ge p\ge|a|$. Define <a id="eq:median-radii"></a>$$\label{eq:median-radii}
 A_+=\frac{p+a}{2},\qquad A_-=\frac{p-a}{2},
 \qquad c=\frac{s-p}{2}.$$ These numbers are nonnegative, with $A_++A_-=p$, $c+A_+=p_1$, and $c+A_-=p_2$.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
###### The compact set of aggregate directions.

<a id="eq:aggregate-set"></a>
Let $$\label{eq:aggregate-set}
 D_0=\{r_+-r_-:\ r_++r_-=x-y,\quad
                  \|r_+\|\le A_+,\quad\|r_-\|\le A_-\}.$$ Our goal is to prove $x+y\in D_0+(s-p)Q$. Such membership supplies $r_+,r_-$ for which $m=x-r_+=y+r_-$ has distances to $0,x,y$ bounded by $c,A_+,A_-$, respectively; the triangle inequalities then force the three median equalities. We shall prove this membership by estimating support functions in each direction.

The set $D_0$ is compact and convex: it is the linear image of the intersection of $A_+Q\times A_-Q$ with the affine subspace $r_++r_-=x-y$. It is nonempty, since $r_\pm=(A_\pm/p)(x-y)$ satisfy the constraints. This also covers $A_+=0$ or $A_-=0$.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
###### Endpoint comparison.

Fix $u\in\operatorname{int}K$ and let $X_\delta=(\delta u,1-\delta)$, $0<\delta<1$. The lifted body $B=K\oplus_1[-1,1]$ contains $X_\delta$ in its interior because $$p_B(X_\delta)=\delta p_K(u)+1-\delta<1.$$ Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:optimal-representation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:optimal-representation">13</a> with this $X_\delta$ and $(q,a)=(x-y,p_1-p_2)$. Its total weight is $T=\max\{\|q\|,|a|\}=p$. We obtain rows $(b_i,\sigma_i)\in Q\times\{-1,1\}$ and weights $\alpha_i\ge0$ satisfying <a href="#eq:optimal-representation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:optimal-representation">[eq:optimal-representation]</a> and <a href="#eq:optimal-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:optimal-cost">[eq:optimal-cost]</a>. Their aggregates $$r_\pm=\sum_{\sigma_i=\pm1}\alpha_i b_i$$ have $r_++r_-=x-y$. Since $\sum_{\sigma_i=\pm1}\alpha_i=(p\pm a)/2=A_\pm$, they satisfy $\|r_\pm\|\le A_\pm$ and hence $r_+-r_-\in D_0$. No choice of these representations across different $u$ or $\delta$ is required.

<a id="eq:median-endpoint-error"></a>
<a id="eq:median-support-interior"></a>
For $b\in Q$ and $\sigma=\pm1$, evenness of $\lambda$ gives $$\lambda\bigl(\langle(b,\sigma),X_\delta\rangle\bigr)
 =\lambda\bigl(1-\delta(1-\sigma\langle b,u\rangle)\bigr).$$ The factors $1-\sigma\langle b,u\rangle$ belong to $[1-p_K(u),1+p_K(u)]$, a compact subinterval of $(0,\infty)$. Lemma <a href="#lem:lens-endpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lens-endpoint">7</a> therefore gives an error $\varepsilon_u(\delta)\to0$ such that $$\label{eq:median-endpoint-error}
 \left|
 \frac{\lambda(\langle(b,\sigma),X_\delta\rangle)}
      {\lambda(1-\delta)}
       -1+\sigma\langle b,u\rangle
 \right|\le\varepsilon_u(\delta)
 \quad(b\in Q,\ \sigma=\pm1).$$ In particular, the normalized cost of the optimal representation is at least $$p-\langle r_+-r_-,u\rangle-p\varepsilon_u(\delta)
 \ge p-h_{D_0}(u)-p\varepsilon_u(\delta).$$ Compare this cost, using <a href="#eq:optimal-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:optimal-cost">[eq:optimal-cost]</a>, with the two-term representation $$(x-y,a)=p_1(x/p_1,1)+p_2(-y/p_2,-1).$$ Both rows belong to $Q\times\{-1,1\}$. By <a href="#eq:median-endpoint-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:median-endpoint-error">[eq:median-endpoint-error]</a>, their normalized cost tends to $s-\langle x+y,u\rangle$. Letting $\delta\downarrow0$ yields $$\label{eq:median-support-interior}
 \langle x+y,u\rangle\le h_{D_0}(u)+s-p
 \qquad(u\in\operatorname{int}K).$$ The error was uniform only over $b,\sigma$ for the fixed $u$; that is all this limit requires.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
###### Separation and the median.

<a id="eq:median-membership"></a>
Continuity extends <a href="#eq:median-support-interior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:median-support-interior">[eq:median-support-interior]</a> to $u\in K$. For any nonzero $v\in\mathbb R^n$, the point $u=v/h_Q(v)$ lies on $\partial K$, by <a href="#eq:gauge-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gauge-support">[eq:gauge-support]</a>. Homogeneity of support functions gives $$\langle x+y,v\rangle\le h_{D_0}(v)+(s-p)h_Q(v).$$ The same inequality holds at $v=0$. The right side is the support function of the compact convex set $D_0+(s-p)Q$, with the convention $0Q=\{0\}$. Convex separation therefore implies $$\label{eq:median-membership}
 x+y\in D_0+(s-p)Q.$$ Choose the corresponding pair $r_+,r_-$ in <a href="#eq:aggregate-set" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:aggregate-set">[eq:aggregate-set]</a>, and put $$m=x-r_+=y+r_-.$$ Then $2m=x+y-(r_+-r_-)$, so <a href="#eq:median-membership" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:median-membership">[eq:median-membership]</a> gives $$\|m\|\le c,\qquad
 \|x-m\|\le A_+,\qquad
 \|y-m\|\le A_-.$$ The triangle inequality forces all three bounds to be equalities: $$\begin{aligned}
 p_1&\le\|m\|+\|x-m\|\le c+A_+=p_1,\\
 p_2&\le\|m\|+\|y-m\|\le c+A_-=p_2.
 \end{aligned}$$ Thus $m$ lies between $0,x$ and between $0,y$, and $$\|x-m\|+\|m-y\|=A_++A_-=p=\|x-y\|.$$ This proves the median property. The argument permits $c=0$ or either $A_\pm=0$, so saturated triangle inequalities require no limiting argument. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Three balls and the equality classification

A normed space has the *three-ball intersection property*, also called the *3.2 intersection property*, if any three closed balls that intersect pairwise have a common point. Their radii may differ. The equivalence between this intersection property and the metric-median condition is Lima’s classical norm-additive decomposition criterion (Lima 1978, Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[Lima1978](#ref-Lima1978)]; see also Hansen–Lima (Hansen and Lima 1981, Theorem 3.6(4)) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[HansenLima1981](#ref-HansenLima1981)]. We include the short direction needed here, with arbitrary radii.

<a id="stmt-lem-three-balls"></a>
<div id="lem:three-balls" class="lemma">

**Lemma 15** (From medians to three balls). *Suppose every triple of points in a real normed space has a metric median. Then the space has the three-ball intersection property, including balls of radius zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-three-balls"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-three-balls</summary>

*Proof.* Consider pairwise-intersecting balls with centers $x_1,x_2,x_3$ and radii $r_1,r_2,r_3\ge0$. Let $m$ be a metric median of their centers, and set $d_i=\|m-x_i\|$. Pairwise intersection and the median property imply $$d_i+d_j=\|x_i-x_j\|\le r_i+r_j
 \qquad(i\ne j).$$ If every $d_i\le r_i$, the median is a common point. Otherwise, after relabeling, $t=d_1-r_1>0$. For $j=2,3$ the displayed inequalities give $d_j+t\le r_j$, so no other ball excludes $m$. Since $0<t\le d_1$, the point $$z=m+\frac{t}{d_1}(x_1-m)$$ is well defined and lies on the segment from $m$ to $x_1$. It satisfies $$\begin{aligned}
 \|z-x_1\|&=d_1-t=r_1,\\
 \|z-x_j\|&\le\|z-m\|+d_j=t+d_j\le r_j\quad(j=2,3).
 \end{aligned}$$ Thus $z$ lies in all three balls. This includes $r_1=0$, when $z=x_1$. ◻

</details>

The required structure theorem is due to Hansen and Lima. In their terminology the balls are closed and have arbitrary radii (Hansen and Lima 1981, sec. 1) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[HansenLima1981](#ref-HansenLima1981)].

<a id="stmt-thm-hansen-lima"></a>
<div id="thm:hansen-lima" class="theorem">

**Theorem 16** (Hansen–Lima). *A nonzero finite-dimensional real Banach space with the 3.2 intersection property is linearly isometric to a space obtained from the real line by finitely many $\ell_1$ and $\ell_\infty$ direct sums.*

</div>

This is (Hansen and Lima 1981, Corollary 7.4) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[HansenLima1981](#ref-HansenLima1981)]. The two direct-sum norms are exactly those in <a href="#eq:sum-gauges" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sum-gauges">[eq:sum-gauges]</a>, so their unit balls are Hanner polytopes.

<a id="stmt-prop-equality-classification"></a>
<div id="prop:equality-classification" class="proposition">

**Proposition 17** (Classification of equality). *If an origin-symmetric convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$, $n\ge1$, satisfies $P(K)=4^n/n!$, then it is a linear Hanner body.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-equality-classification"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-equality-classification</summary>

*Proof.* Put $Q=K^\circ$. Proposition <a href="#prop:metric-median" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:metric-median">14</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:three-balls" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:three-balls">15</a> give the 3.2 intersection property for $(\mathbb R^n,p_Q)$. This is a real Banach space, since every finite-dimensional normed space is complete. Theorem <a href="#thm:hansen-lima" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:hansen-lima">16</a> gives an invertible linear map $T$ and a Hanner polytope $H$ with $Q=TH$. By <a href="#eq:linear-polarity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:linear-polarity">[eq:linear-polarity]</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:hanner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hanner">5</a>, $$K=Q^\circ=T^{-\mathsf T}H^\circ$$ is a linear Hanner body. The converse was proved in Lemma <a href="#lem:hanner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hanner">5</a>; together with Corollary <a href="#cor:symmetric-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:symmetric-inequality">12</a>, this completes Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The conformal lens and its boundary coordinate. Equally spaced angles on either semicircle give equally spaced imaginary coordinates on the corresponding boundary branch. On the right semicircle, $F(e^{is}) = λ(2s/π) + 2is/π$; reflection gives the left branch. Thus a uniform angle gives a uniform coordinate in $[−1,1]$ and an independent fair branch sign. The horizontal arrow marks the half-width $λ(t_{0})$, with $s_{0} = π/6$ and $t_{0} = 1/3$.",
      "figure_id": "fig:lens-boundary-law",
      "pdf_page": 9,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:lens-boundary-law",
          "exact_quote": "The conformal lens and its boundary coordinate. Equally spaced\nangles on either semicircle give equally spaced imaginary coordinates\non the corresponding boundary branch. On the right semicircle,\n$F(e^{is})=\\lambda(2s/\\pi)+2is/\\pi$; reflect",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "other",
      "tikz_pgf_source": null,
      "witness_description": "Two diagrams side by side: (a) labelled \"(a) Equal angular steps\" showing the unit disk $\\mathbb{D}$ in the complex plane with axes $\\Re z$ and $+\\Im z$, an angle $s_0$ from the real axis, and point $e^{i s_0}$ on the right semicircle; a horizontal arrow labelled $F$ with $t_0 = 2s_0/\\pi$ points from (a) to (b); (b) labelled \"(b) Equal vertical steps\" showing the conformal lens domain $D$ with vertical axis $t$ (marked at $1$, $t_0$, $-1$) and horizontal axis $v$, with a horizontal arrow from the vertical axis at $t_0$ to the right boundary curve labelled $\\lambda(t_0)$. Centered below both diagrams is the formula $F(e^{is}) = \\lambda(2s/\\pi) + i 2s/\\pi \\quad (-\\pi/2 < s < \\pi/2)$."
    },
    {
      "caption": "A feasible-simplex union can leave part of the polar uncovered. Here $b_{1} = (1,0)$, $b_2=(1/2,\\sqrt3/2)$, $b_3=(-1/2,\\sqrt3/2)$, $C = conv { ± b_{1}, ± b_{2}, ± b_{3}}$, and $X=0.9(1,1/\\sqrt3)$. The third strip is redundant in $L_{X}$, as verified above. The four witnesses $Y_{ϵ1}ϵ_{2}$, defined by $b_{i}Y = ϵ_{i}a$ for $i = 1, 2$, correspond to the four triangles $conv (0,ϵ_{1}b_{1},ϵ_{2}b_{2})$ with matching sign labels. Their union is $Σ_{X} = conv { ± b_{1}, ± b_{2}}$. The two hatched caps have total area $\\sqrt3/2$, one third of $|C|$.",
      "figure_id": "fig:feasible-simplex-deficit",
      "pdf_page": 17,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:feasible-simplex-deficit",
          "exact_quote": "A feasible-simplex union can leave part of the polar uncovered.\nHere $b_1=(1,0)$, $b_2=(1/2,\\sqrt3/2)$,\n$b_3=(-1/2,\\sqrt3/2)$, $C=\\operatorname{conv}\\{\\pm b_1,\\pm b_2,\\pm b_3\\}$,\nand $X=0.9(1,1/\\sqrt3)$.\nThe third strip is redundant in $L_X",
          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "other",
      "tikz_pgf_source": null,
      "witness_description": "Two diagrams side by side: (a) labelled \"(a) Feasible witnesses in $L_X$\" showing a region $L_X$ between bounding lines $b_2 Y = a$ (upper left) and $b_1 Y = a$ (lower right) with four vertices $Y_{-+}$, $Y_{++}$, $Y_{--}$, and $Y_{+-}$ connected by dashed lines forming a parallelogram; below are the formulas $\\max_{Y \\in L_X} |b_3 Y| = 2a < \\lambda(0)$ and $a = \\lambda(0.9)$; (b) labelled \"(b) Feasible simplices in $C$\" showing a regular hexagon $C$ with vertices $b_1, b_2, b_3, -b_1, -b_2, -b_3$ centered at $0$, containing four shaded triangles meeting at $0$ with sign labels $++$, $-+$, $+-$, $--$ forming parallelogram $\\Sigma_X = \\operatorname{conv}\\{\\pm b_1, \\pm b_2\\}$, and two horizontally hatched caps at top and bottom; below are the expressions $\\Sigma_X = \\operatorname{conv}\\{\\pm b_1, \\pm b_2\\}$ and $|C \\setminus \\Sigma_X| = \\sqrt{3}/2$."
    }
  ],
